\ell_{2}={|{V(F_{2})}|}\geq 2

Also, as
{|{F_{1}}|}\leq{|{F}|}\leq k
and
{|{F_{2}}|}\leq{|{F}|}\leq k
, we have
\ell_{1}={|{V(F_{1})}|}\leq 2k
and
\ell_{2}={|{V(F_{2})}|}\leq 2k
. Thus
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in[2k]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\}
.

We may think of the cluster graph
G\triangle F
as obtained from
G
by a two-step process: first by deleting from
G
the edges in
F_{1}
and then by adding to
G-F_{1}
the edges in
F_{2}
. Let
G^{\prime}=G-F_{1}
. Then
G^{\prime}
is a cluster graph, and we have
G\triangle F=(G-F_{1})+F_{2}=G^{\prime}+F_{2}
.
We can argue that
F_{1}
corresponds to a partition
Y=\left\{{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{s}}\right\}
of
\ell_{1}
and the corresponding multiset
\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\}
, and
F_{2}
corresponds to a partition
X=\left\{{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}}\right\}
of
\ell_{2}
and the corresponding multiset
\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}
of partitions that satisfy the properties required by the lemma.
Intuitively, the partition
Y_{j}=\left\{{y_{j1},y_{j2},\ldots,y_{jq_{j}}}\right\}
of
y_{j}
corresponds to a component of
G
of size exactly
y_{j}
that was split into components of sizes
y_{j1},y_{j2},\ldots,y_{jq_{j}}
by deleting the edges in
F_{1}
. Consequently,
G^{\prime}
is a deletion of
G
with respect to
(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
. Each
X_{i}=\left\{{x_{i1},x_{i2},\ldots,x_{ir_{i}}}\right\}
corresponds to the components of
G^{\prime}
of sizes
x_{i1},x_{i2},\ldots,x_{ir_{i}}
that were merged into a single component of
G^{\prime}+F_{2}=G\triangle F
of size
x_{i}
.
That is,
G^{\prime}+F_{2}=G\triangle F
is a completion of
G^{\prime}
with respect to
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
.
These arguments will also imply that
\sum_{j\in[s]}\mathtt{spp}(Y_{j})={|{F_{1}}|}
and
\sum_{i\in[t]}\mathtt{spp}(X_{i})={|{F_{2}}|}
, and thus
\sum_{j\in[s]}\mathtt{spp}(Y_{j})+\sum_{i\in[t]}\mathtt{spp}(X_{i})={|{F_{1}}|}+{|{F_{2}}|}={|{F}|}\leq k
.
∎

Algorithm for BCE on cluster graphs

We now design an algorithm for
BCE
on cluster graphs, which we call
Algo-BCE-C
. The correctness of the algorithm follows from Lemma
57
.

Algo-BCE-C
.

Given an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
BCE
as input, where
G
is a cluster graph, we proceed as follows.

Step 1.

If
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCC
or a yes-instance of
BCD
, we return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCE
, and terminate. To do this, we use the algorithms of Theorems
54
and
42
.

Step 2.

If
k\leq 0
, then we return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a no-instance, and terminate.

Step 3.

We use the algorithm of Proposition
48
to generate all partitions of
\ell
for all
\ell\in[2k]
.

Step 4.

For every choice of
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in[2k]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\}
, we do as follows.

Step 4.1.

For each choice of partitions
Y
of
\ell_{1}
and
X
of
\ell_{2}
, we do as follows.

Step 4.1.1.

Let
Y=\left\{{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{s}}\right\}
and
X=\left\{{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}}\right\}
. For each choice of multisets
Y=\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\}
and
X=\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}
, where
Y_{j}
is a partition of
y_{j}
for every
j\in[s]
and
X_{i}
is a partition of
x_{i}
for every
i\in[t]
, we do as follows.

Step 4.1.1.1.

If
Y
is
G
-valid, then we construct the graph
G^{\prime}
, where
G^{\prime}
is a deletion of
G
with respect to
(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
, and we consider the partition
X^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in[t]}X_{i}
of
\ell_{2}
. If
X^{\prime}
is
G^{\prime}
-valid, a completion of
G^{\prime}
with respect to
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
is
\eta
-balanced and
\sum_{j\in[s]}\mathtt{spp}(Y_{j})+\sum_{i\in[t]}\mathtt{spp}(X_{i})\leq k
, then we return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance, and terminate.

Step 5.

We return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a no-instance, and terminate.

We now analyse the running time of
Algo-BCE-C
.

Lemma 58
.

Algo-BCE-C
runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

Proof.

Observe that Steps 2 and 5 take only polynomial time.
So does each execution of Step 4.1.1.1.
By Theorems
54
and
42
, Step 1 takes time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. By Proposition
48
, Step 3 takes time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
.
Now, in Step 4, we only need to go over at most
2k-1
choices of
\ell_{1}
and at most
2k-1
choices of
\ell_{2}
; and we can assume without loss of generality that
k\leq\binom{n}{2}
.
That is, there are only
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
choices for the pair
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
. In Step 4.1, we go over all possible choices of
(Y,X)
. As
Y
is a partition of
\ell
, by Proposition
48
,
Y
has
p(\ell_{1})=2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\ell_{1}})}
choices. Similarly,
X
has
p(\ell_{2})=2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\ell_{2}})}
choices. Thus, there are
2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\ell_{1}})}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\ell_{2}})}=2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\ell_{1}}+\sqrt{\ell_{2}})}=2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
choices for the pair
(Y,X)
.
Finally, by Lemma
51
, for each choice of
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
, the number of choices for
((Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\}),(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}))
is at most
2^{\mathcal{O}(\ell_{1})}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\ell_{2})}=2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
. Thus, the overall running time of the algorithm is bounded by
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
∎

This completes the proof of Theorem
56
.

4.4
Faster Algorithms for BCC and BCE

In Section
4.3
, we designed algorithms for
BCC
and
BCE
that run in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. By refining the ideas that we used for these algorithms,
we now design algorithms for
BCC
and
BCE
that run in time
4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

The high level idea.

We use
BCC
to illustrate the idea behind our algorithms; this idea extends to
BCE
as well. Consider a yes-instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
BCC
, and suppose
F\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}\setminus E(G)
is the solution that we are looking for; and
G_{1},G_{2},\ldots,G_{t}
are the components of the
\eta
-balanced cluster graph
G+F
that were modified by
F
, and for each
i\in[t]
,
G_{i1},G_{i2},\ldots,G_{ir_{i}}
are the components of
G
that were merged together to form the component
G_{i}
of
G
. In our previous algorithm for
BCC
(
Algo-BCC
), we guessed
{|{V(F)}|}
; then we guessed
X=\left\{{{|{G_{1}}|},{|{G_{2}}|},\ldots,{|{G_{t}}|}}\right\}
, and for each
i\in[t]
, we guessed
X_{i}=\left\{{{|{G_{i1}}|},{|{G_{i2}}|},\ldots,{|{G_{ir_{i}}}|}}\right\}
. As
X
is a partition of
{|{V(F)}|}\in[2k]
, and as the number of partitions of any integer
\ell
is
2^{o(\ell)}
(Proposition
48
), we can guess
X
in time
2^{o(k)}
. But guessing each
X_{i}
is what led to the
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
factor in the running time (Lemma
51
); we now design a faster algorithm by avoiding this time-consuming guess. To do this, we guess
{|{V(F)}|}
and
X
as before, and we guess
X^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in[t]}X_{i}
instead of guessing each
X_{i}
separately. Notice that as
X
and
X^{\prime}
are partitions of
{|{V(F)}|}\in[2k]
, we only make
2k\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}=2^{o(k)}
guesses. Once we make our guesses, we check in time
2^{2k}=4^{k}
if we can merge the components by adding at most
k
edges; this is the more non-trivial step of our algorithm.

To check if we can merge the components, we think of the components
G_{1},G_{2},\ldots,G_{t}
of
G+F
as bins, and for all
i\in[t]
the components
G_{i1},G_{i2},\ldots,G_{ir_{i}}
of
G
as balls, where the size a ball (or the capacity of a bin) is the size of the corresponding component. And in the balls-and-bins parlance, merging the components of
G
to form the components of
G+F
simply means assigning balls to bins so that the total size of balls assigned to each bin does not exceed the capacity of that bin. Also, notice that to assign a ball of size
{|{G_{ij}}|}
to a bin of capacity
{|{G_{i}}|}
, we need to add exactly
{|{G_{ij}}|}({|{G_{i}}|}-{|{G_{ij}}|})
edges incident with
V(G_{ij})
; we think of this quantity as the cost of assigning this ball to this bin. So guessing the partitions
X
and
X^{\prime}
means guessing the capacities of the bins and the sizes of the balls. And for each
(X,X^{\prime})
, we simply need to check if we can assign the balls to the bins subject to capacity and cost constraints; we discuss below how we can do this in time
4^{k}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. Notice that as
X
and
X^{\prime}
are partitions of
{|{V(F)}|}\leq 2k
, we have
{|{X}|},{|{X^{\prime}}|}\leq 2k
; that is, we have at most
2k
balls and at most
2k
bins.
\lipicsEnd

To exploit these ideas and design our algorithms for
BCC
and
BCE
, we first define an auxiliary problem called
Balls in Bins
(
B-in-B
) and design an algorithm for
B-in-B
. We then show that we can reduce
BCC
and
BCE
to
B-in-B
.

4.4.1
An Algorithm for
Balls in Bins

Consider a set of
s
balls (indexed by
1,2,\ldots,s
) with sizes
b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{s}\in\mathbb{N}
, a set of
t
bins (indexed by
1,2,\ldots,t
) with capacities
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\in\mathbb{N}
. We are interested in assigning these balls to the bins in such a way that for each bin
j
, the sum of the sizes of balls assigned to bin
j
does not exceed the capacity of that bin. Formally, consider
B\subseteq[s]
. We define the volume of
B
, denoted by
\mathtt{vol}(B)
, to be the sum of the sizes of the balls in
B
, i.e.,
\mathtt{vol}(B)=\sum_{i\in B}b_{i}
if
B\neq\emptyset
and
\mathtt{vol}(B)=0
if
B=\emptyset
.
By an assignment of the balls in
B
to the
t
bins (or simply an assignment of
B
, for short), we mean a function
{{\beta}:{B}\rightarrow{[t]}}
such that
\mathtt{vol}(\beta^{-1}(j))\leq x_{j}
for every
j\in[t]
. If
\beta(i)=j
for
i\in[s]
and
j\in[t]
, then we say that ball
i
is assigned to bin
j
(or that the
i
th ball is assigned to the
j
th bin) under the assignment
\beta
. Now, consider a cost function
{{\mathtt{cost}}:{[s]\times[t]}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}}}
, where for every
i\in[s]
and
j\in[t]
,
\mathtt{cost}(i,j)
is the cost of assigning the
i
th ball to the
j
th bin.
For
B^{\prime}\subseteq B\subseteq[s]
, and an assignment
{{\beta}:{B}\rightarrow{[t]}}
, the cost of
B^{\prime}
under the assignment
\beta
is
\sum_{i\in B^{\prime}}\mathtt{cost}(i,\beta(i))
, and we denote this quantity by
\mathtt{cost}_{\beta}(B^{\prime})
; when the assignment
\beta
is clear from the context, we may omit the subscript and simply write
\mathtt{cost}(B^{\prime})
.

We now define the following problem.

Input:

A set of
s
balls (indexed by
1,2,\ldots,s
) with sizes
b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{s}\in\mathbb{N}
, a set of
t
bins (indexed by
1,2,\ldots,t
) with capacities
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}\in\mathbb{N}
, a budget
W\in\mathbb{N}
, and a cost function
{{\mathtt{cost}}:{[s]\times[t]}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}\cup\left\{{0}\right\}}}
where
\mathtt{cost}(i,j)
is the cost of assigning the
i
th ball to the
j
th bin for every
i\in[s]
and
j\in[t]
.

Task:

Decide if there exists an assignment of the balls in
[s]
to the
t
bins such that the cost of the assignment is at most
W
.

Balls in Bins (B-in-B)

Observe that we can design a straightforward dynamic programming algorithm for
B-in-B
that runs in time
\mathcal{O}^{\star}(3^{s})
(assuming
\mathtt{cost}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
W
are encoded in unary): for each subset
B\subseteq[s]
,
j\in[t]
and
q\in[W]_{0}
, we simply need to check if there is an assignment of
B
to the first
j
bins with cost at most
q
. To execute this, we need to go over all subsets
B
of
[s]
, and check if there exist a subset
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
and
q^{\prime}\leq q
such that we can assign
B^{\prime}
to the
j
th bin with cost at most
q^{\prime}
and assign
B\setminus B^{\prime}
to the first
j-1
bins with cost at most
q-q^{\prime}
, which will take time
\mathcal{O}^{\star}(\sum_{B\subseteq[s]}2^{{|{B}|}})=\mathcal{O}^{\star}(\sum_{i=0}^{s}\binom{s}{i}2^{i})=\mathcal{O}^{\star}(3^{s})
. We now show that we can execute this idea in time
\mathcal{O}^{\star}(2^{s})
by using fast polynomial multiplication.
8
8

8

We can also use the fast subset convolution (FSC) algorithm of Björklund et al.
[
9
]
to accelerate this DP to run in time
\mathcal{O}^{\star}(2^{s})
. But instead of using FSC, we resort to fast Fourier transform (FFT)-based polynomial multiplication to achieve the same running time. While both tools guarantee the running time of
\mathcal{O}^{\star}(2^{s})
, FFT is arguably preferable to FSC when it comes to implementation.
As noted by Cygan and Pilipczuk
[
20
]
, FFT is widely known in the computer science community, with a number of libraries readily available for its implementation
[
28
]
. Also, FSC relies on Möbius transforms, which requires repeated additions and subtractions, and this can lead to large rounding errors in floating point arithmetic
[
42
]
.
Specifically, we prove the following theorem.
9
9

9

Balls in Bins
is a special case of the the
Generalised Assignment Problem (GAP)
, which is often phrased in terms of assigning jobs to machines. While GAP has been studied intensively in the approximation algorithms framework, where the typical goals have been minimising the cost and makespan (the maximum time taken by any machine to complete the jobs assigned to it), we are unaware of an exact exponential time algorithm for GAP, along the lines of our algorithm in Theorem
59
. We refer the reader to Williamson and Shmoys
[
57
, Chapter 11]
for approximation algorithms for GAP and its variants; and to Kundakcioglu and Alizamir
[
44
]
for an overview of literature on GAP.

Theorem 59
.

Balls in Bins
admits an algorithm that runs in time
\mathcal{O}(2^{s}\cdot s^{3}\cdot t\cdot W^{2})
.

To prove Theorem
59
, we rely on the fact that we can efficiently multiply two polynomials.

Proposition 60

(
[
49
]
)
.

The product of two polynomials of degree
d
can be computed in time
\mathcal{O}(d\log d)
.

We now introduce the following notation and terminology, which we borrow from
[
20
,
35
]
.

Notation and Terminology.

Consider
B\subseteq[s]
. The characteristic vector of
B
, denoted by
\chi(B)
, is the
s
-length binary string whose
i
th bit is
1
if and only if
i\in B
. The Hamming weight of a binary string is the number of
1
s in that string. Notice that we can interpret a binary string as the binary encoding of an integer and vice-versa. For example, we interpret the string
(1,0,0,1,0)
to be the number 18 as the binary encoding of 18 is exactly
10010
. For
B\subseteq[s]
, consider the monomial
z^{\chi(B)}
, where
z
is an indeterminate and
\chi(B)
is the characteristic vector of
B
, interpreted as the binary encoding of an integer. The Hamming weight of a monomial
z^{i}
is the Hamming weight of the binary encoding (interpreted as a binary string) of
i
. Consider a polynomial
P(z)
and an integer
i
. The Hamming projection of
P(z)
to
i
, denoted by
\mathcal{H}_{i}(P(z))
, is the sum of the monomials of
P
of Hamming weight exactly
i
. That is,
\mathcal{H}_{i}(P(z))
is the polynomial obtained from
P(z)
by removing all those monomials of Hamming weight not equal to
i
. In particular, if
P(z)
has no monomial of Hamming weight exactly
i
, then
\mathcal{H}_{i}(P(z))
is the zero polynomial. Finally, we define the representative polynomial of
P(z)
, denoted by
\mathcal{R}(P(z))
, to be the polynomial obtained from
P(z)
by replacing the coefficient of each (non-zero) monomial with
1
. For example, consider the polynomial
P(z)=z^{8}+2z^{6}+z^{5}+3z^{2}+9
. Then
\mathcal{H}_{2}(P(z))=2z^{6}+z^{5}
as the binary encodings of 6 and 5 respectively are 0110 and 0101, and they both have Hamming weight 2, whereas the binary encodings of
8,2
and
0
(the monomial
9
is
9z^{0}
) respectively are 1000, 0010 and 0000, none of which has Hamming weight 2. And
\mathcal{R}(P(z))=z^{8}+z^{6}+z^{5}+z^{2}+1
.

Outline of our algorithm for
B-in-B
.

Consider an instance of
B-in-B
with
s
balls,
t
bins and budget
W
. The idea is to encode all possible assignments of balls in
[s]
to the
t
bins as a polynomial, each monomial of which will correspond to an assignment. More specifically, for each
i\in[s]_{0},j\in[t],q\in[W]_{0}
, we define a polynomial
P_{i,j,q}(z)
, where each monomial of
P_{i,j,q}(z)
will correspond to an assignment of a subset of exactly
i
balls to the first
j
bins so that the total cost of this assignment is at most
q
(if at least one such assignment exists, in which case each monomial will have Hamming weight exactly
i
; otherwise,
P_{i,j,q}(z)
will just be the zero polynomial). In particular, for
B\subseteq[s]
with
{|{B}|}=i
, the polynomial
P_{i,j,q}(z)
will contain the monomial
z^{\chi(B)}
if and only if there is an assignment of
B
to the first
j
bins with cost at most
q
. We compute these polynomials iteratively by going from smaller to larger values of
j
. The polynomial that we ultimately need to compute is
P_{s,t,W}(z)
, and we will have the guarantee that the given instance of
B-in-B
is a yes-instance if and only if
P_{s,t,W}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi([s])}
.
\lipicsEnd

Before proceeding to the proof of Theorem
59
, we state one crucial result that we will rely on to establish the correctness of our algorithm. In our algorithm, we only check if there is an assignment of the balls to the bins; we do not guess which subset of balls is assigned to which bin. We must nonetheless ensure that the subsets of balls assigned to different bins are indeed disjoint. For that, we use the following result, which hardwires this disjointness requirement into the the polynomial
P_{i,j,q}(z)
that we will define.

Proposition 61

(
[
20
]
)
.

Let
B_{1},B_{2}\subseteq[s]
. Then
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are disjoint if and only if the Hamming weight of the monomial
z^{\chi(B_{1})+\chi(B_{2})}
is
{|{B_{1}}|}+{|{B_{2}}|}
.

We are now ready to prove Theorem
59
.

Proof of Theorem
59
.

Consider an instance of
B-in-B
, where we are given
s
balls (indexed by
1,2,\ldots,s
) with sizes
b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{s}
,
t
bins (indexed by
1,2,\ldots,t
) with capacities
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}
, a cost function
{{\mathtt{cost}}:{[s]\times[t]}\rightarrow{\mathbb{N}\cup\left\{{0}\right\}}}
and a budget
W\in\mathbb{N}
.

For each
i\in[s]_{0},j\in[t],q\in[W]_{0}
, we will define a polynomial
P_{i,j,q}(z)
, where each monomial of
P_{i,j,q}(z)
will correspond to a (partial) assignment of a set of exactly
i
balls to the first
j
bins such that the cost of the assignment is at most
q
. To that end, we will first define an auxiliary polynomial
A_{i,j,q}(z)
, each monomial of which will correspond to an assignment of a set of exactly
i
balls to the
j
th bin such that the cost of the assignment is at most
q
.
Formally, for every
i\in[s]_{0},j\in[t],q\in[W]_{0}
, we define

A_{i,j,q}(z)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\subseteq[s]\\
{|{B}|}=i\\
\mathtt{vol}(B)\leq x_{j}\\
\sum_{r\in B}\mathtt{cost}(r,j)\leq q\end{subarray}}z^{\chi(B)}.

Notice that
A_{i,j,q}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi(B)}
if and only if
{|{B}|}=i
and the balls in
B
can be assigned to the
j
th bin with cost at most
q
. We now define
P_{i,j,q}(z)
for every
i\in[s]_{0},j\in[t],q\in[W]_{0}
as follows. We define
P_{i,1,q}(z)=A_{i,1,q}(z)
. For
j>1
, notice that there exists an assignment of
i
balls to the first
j
bins with cost at most
q
if and only if there exist an assignment of exactly
i^{\prime}
balls to
j
th bin with cost at most
q^{\prime}
and an assignment of
i-i^{\prime}
balls to the first
j-1
bins with cost at most
q-q^{\prime}
, for some
i^{\prime}\in[i]_{0}
and
q^{\prime}\in[q]_{0}
. We thus define

P_{i,j,q}(z)=\mathcal{R}\left({\mathcal{H}_{i}\left({\sum_{(i^{\prime},q^{\prime})\in[i]_{0}\times[q]_{0}}A_{i^{\prime},j,q^{\prime}}(z)\cdot P_{i-i^{\prime},j-1,q-q^{\prime}}(z)}\right)}\right),

(
\star\star\star
)

where
\mathcal{H}_{i}(\cdot)
is the Hamming projection to
i
and
\mathcal{R}(\cdot)
is the representative polynomial. The
\mathcal{H}_{i}(\cdot)
operator ensures that
P_{i,j,q}(z)
contains only monomials of Hamming weight exactly
i
(which correspond to assignments of exactly
i
balls). And the
\mathcal{R}(\cdot)
operator ensures that the coefficients of the polynomials we multiply always remain
1
. In particular, the coefficient of each (non-zero) monomial of
P_{i,j,q}(z)
(and
A_{i,j,q}(z)
) is
1
.

We first compute
A_{i,j,q}(z)
for every
i\in[s]_{0},j\in[t]
and
q\in[W]_{0}
by going over all subsets
B\subseteq[s]
. We then compute
P_{i,j,q}(z)
using Equation (

⋆
⋆
⋆

‣
4.4.1
). In particular, we use the algorithm of Proposition
60
to compute the product
A_{i^{\prime},j,q^{\prime}}(z)\cdot P_{i-i^{\prime},j-1,q-q^{\prime}}(z)
in Equation (

⋆
⋆
⋆

‣
4.4.1
) for every choice of
(i^{\prime},q^{\prime})
. Finally, we return that the given instance of
B-in-B
is a yes-instance if and only if the polynomial
P_{s,t,W}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi([s])}
. (Notice that if the given instance of
B-in-B
is a yes-instance, then we can find an assignment of
[s]
with cost at most
W
by backtracking from
P_{s,t,W}(z)
.)

Let us now analyse the running time of our algorithm. Since
i\in[s]_{0},j\in[t]
and
q\in[W]_{0}
, we compute
\mathcal{O}(s\cdot t\cdot W)
polynomials. Each polynomial has degree at most
2^{s}
as the number represented by any
s
-length binary string is at most
2^{s}
. Also, to compute
P_{i,j,q}(z)
using Equation (

⋆
⋆
⋆

‣
4.4.1
), we go over
\mathcal{O}(s\cdot W)
choices of
(i^{\prime},q^{\prime})
, and for each such choice, multiply two polynomials of degree at most
2^{s}
; by Proposition
60
, we can multiply two polynomials of degree at most
2^{s}
in time
\mathcal{O}(2^{s}\cdot s)
. Finally, notice that we can apply the operators
\mathcal{R}(\cdot)
and
\mathcal{H}_{i}(\cdot)
in time proportional to
2^{s}
. Hence the total running time is bounded by
\mathcal{O}((s\cdot t\cdot W)\cdot(s\cdot W)\cdot(2^{s}\cdot s))=\mathcal{O}(2^{s}\cdot s^{3}\cdot t\cdot W^{2})
.

Finally, to establish the correctness of our algorithm, we prove the following claim.

Claim 62
.

Consider
i\in[s],j\in[t]
and
q\in[W]_{0}
.
For every
B\subseteq[s]
with
{|{B}|}=i
, there exists an assignment
{{\beta}:{B}\rightarrow{[j]}}
with
\mathtt{cost}_{\beta}(B)\leq q
if and only if the polynomial
P_{i,j,q}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi(B)}
.

Assuming for now that Claim
62
holds, let us complete the proof of the theorem.
Recall that our algorithm returns yes if and only if the polynomial
P_{s,t,W}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi([s])}
, which by Claim
62
, holds if and only if there is an assignment of
[s]
with cost at most
W
. We can thus conclude that our algorithm returns yes if and only if there is an assignment of
[s]
with cost at most
W
.

{claimproof}

[Proof of Claim
62
]
Fix
B\subseteq[s]
with
{|{B}|}=i
. We prove the claim by induction on
j
.

Consider
j=1
. Observe that there exists an assignment
\beta
that assigns
B
to the first bin with cost at most
q
if and only if
\mathtt{vol}(B)\leq x_{1}
and
\mathtt{cost}_{\beta}(B)=\sum_{r\in B}\mathtt{cost}(r,1)\leq q
, which, by the definition of
A_{i,1,q}(z)
, holds if and only if the polynomial
A_{i,1,q}(z)=P_{i,1,q}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi(B)}
.

Assume now that the claim holds for
j=j^{\prime}-1
. We prove that it holds for
j=j^{\prime}
.

Suppose that there exists an assignment
{{\beta}:{B}\rightarrow{[j^{\prime}]}}
with
\mathtt{cost}(B)\leq q
. Let
\hat{B}\subseteq B
be the set of balls that are assigned to bin
j^{\prime}
under
\beta
. Let
\hat{i}={|{\hat{B}}|}
and
\hat{q}=\mathtt{cost}_{\beta}(\hat{B})
. First, as the
\hat{i}
balls in
\hat{B}
are assigned to bin
j^{\prime}
under
\beta
with cost
\hat{q}
, the polynomial
A_{\hat{i},j^{\prime},\hat{q}}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi(\hat{B})}
.
Second, by the definition of
\hat{B}
, the
i-\hat{i}={|{B\setminus\hat{B}}|}
balls in
B\setminus\hat{B}
are assigned to the first
j^{\prime}-1
balls under
\beta
with cost
\mathtt{cost}(B)-\mathtt{cost}(\hat{B})\leq q-\hat{q}
.
Hence, by the induction hypothesis, the polynomial
P_{i-\hat{i},j^{\prime}-1,q-\hat{q}}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi(B\setminus\hat{B})}
.
Therefore the product
A_{\hat{i},j^{\prime},\hat{q}}(z)\cdot P_{i-\hat{i},j^{\prime}-1,q-\hat{q}}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi(\hat{B})+\chi(B\setminus\hat{B})}
.
As
{|{\hat{B}}|}+{|{B\setminus\hat{B}}|}=i
and
\mathtt{cost}(\hat{B})+\mathtt{cost}(B\setminus\hat{B})=\mathtt{cost}(B)\leq q
, the sum on the right hand side of Equation (

⋆
⋆
⋆

‣
4.4.1
) contains the summand
A_{\hat{i},j^{\prime},\hat{q}}(z)\cdot P_{i-\hat{i},j^{\prime}-1,q-\hat{q}}(z)
, and hence the monomial
z^{\chi(\hat{B})+\chi(B\setminus\hat{B})}
.
Finally, as the sets
\hat{B}
and
B\setminus\hat{B}
are disjoint, by Proposition
61
, the monomial
z^{\chi(\hat{B})+\chi(B\setminus\hat{B})}
has Hamming weight exactly
{|{\hat{B}}|}+{|{B\setminus\hat{B}}|}={|{B}|}=i
. And applying the operators
\mathcal{H}_{i}(\cdot)
and
\mathcal{R}(\cdot)
does not change the Hamming weight of this monomial.
Finally, notice again that as the sets
\hat{B}
and
B\setminus\hat{B}
are disjoint,
\chi(\hat{B})+\chi(B\setminus\hat{B})
(interpreted as a binary sum) is precisely
\chi(\hat{B}\cup(B\setminus\hat{B}))=\chi(B)
.
We can thus conclude that
P_{i,j^{\prime},q}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi(B)}
.

Conversely, assume that the polynomial
P_{i,j^{\prime},q}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi(B)}
.
Then, by Equation (

⋆
⋆
⋆

‣
4.4.1
), there exist
i^{\prime}\in[i]_{0}
and
q^{\prime}\in[q]_{0}
such that the product
A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(z)\cdot P_{i-i^{\prime},j^{\prime}-1,q-q^{\prime}}(z)
contains the monomial
z^{\chi(B)}
. For convenience, let
i^{\prime\prime}=i-i^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime\prime}=q-q^{\prime}
.
Let
z^{a}
be a monomial of
A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(z)
and
z^{p}
be a monomial of
P_{i^{\prime\prime},j-1,q^{\prime\prime}}
such that
z^{a}\cdot z^{p}=z^{a+p}=z^{\chi(B)}
.
Notice that every monomial in
A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(z)
has Hamming weight exactly
i^{\prime}
and every monomial in
P_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}-1,q^{\prime\prime}}(z)
has Hamming weight exactly
i^{\prime\prime}
.
We can therefore conclude that the monomials
z^{a}
and
z^{p}
have Hamming weights
i^{\prime}
and
i^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.

We will now show that the monomials
z^{a}
and
z^{p}
respectively correspond to assignments of subsets
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
and
B^{\prime\prime}\subseteq[B]
with
B^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime\prime}=B
and
B^{\prime}\cap B^{\prime\prime}=\emptyset
. First, by the definition of
A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(z)
, each monomial of
A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(z)
corresponds to an assignment of a set of exactly
i^{\prime}
balls to bin
j^{\prime}
with cost at most
q^{\prime}
.
Let
B^{\prime}\subseteq[s]
with
{|{B^{\prime}}|}=i^{\prime}
be the set of exactly
i^{\prime}
balls such that the monomial
z^{a}
of
A_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(z)
corresponds to the assignment of
B^{\prime}
to bin
j^{\prime}
(with cost at most
q^{\prime}
); let this assignment be denoted by
\beta^{\prime}
.
In particular, we have
z^{a}=z^{\chi(B^{\prime})}
. Second, let
B^{\prime\prime}\subseteq[s]
be such that
\chi(B^{\prime\prime})
(interpreted as the binary encoding of an integer) is precisely
p
. That is,
z^{p}=z^{\chi(B^{\prime\prime})}
. Notice that such a set
B^{\prime\prime}
exists as the monomial
z^{p}
(and in particular, the binary encoding of
p
) has Hamming weight
i^{\prime\prime}\leq s
. Because of the same reason, we can conclude that
{|{B^{\prime\prime}}|}=i^{\prime\prime}
. We thus have
B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}\subseteq[s]
with
{|{B^{\prime}}|}=i^{\prime}
,
{|{B^{\prime\prime}}|}=i^{\prime\prime}=i-i^{\prime}
and
z^{\chi(B^{\prime})}\cdot z^{\chi(B^{\prime\prime})}=z^{\chi(B^{\prime})+\chi(B^{\prime\prime})}=z^{\chi(B)}
. As the monomial
z^{\chi(B)}=z^{\chi(B^{\prime})+\chi(B^{\prime\prime})}
has Hamming weight
{|{B}|}=i=i^{\prime}+i^{\prime\prime}={|{B^{\prime}}|}+{|{B^{\prime\prime}}|}
, by Proposition
61
, we can conclude that the sets
B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime\prime}
are disjoint. Observe then that we must have
B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}\subseteq B
, for otherwise we would not have
\chi(B^{\prime})+\chi(B^{\prime\prime})=\chi(B)
. As
{|{B^{\prime\prime}}|}=i^{\prime\prime}=i-i^{\prime}={|{B}|}-{|{B^{\prime}}|}
, we can conclude further that
B^{\prime\prime}=B\setminus B^{\prime}
.
We have thus argued that the polynomial
P_{i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime}-1,q^{\prime\prime}}
contains the monomial
z^{p}=z^{\chi(B^{\prime\prime})}
. By the induction hypothesis, there exists an assignment
{{\beta^{\prime\prime}}:{B^{\prime\prime}}\rightarrow{[j^{\prime}-1]}}
of
B^{\prime\prime}
with
\mathtt{cost}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}(B^{\prime\prime})\leq q^{\prime\prime}
.

Let
{{\gamma}:{B}\rightarrow{[}}j^{\prime}]
be the assignment of
B
obtained by combining
\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime\prime}
as follows: We have
\gamma(\tilde{i})=\beta^{\prime}(\tilde{i})=j^{\prime}
if
\tilde{i}\in B^{\prime}
and
\gamma(\tilde{i})=\beta^{\prime\prime}(\tilde{i})
if
\tilde{i}\in B^{\prime\prime}
. As
B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime\prime}
are disjoint and
B^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime\prime}=B
, the assignment
\gamma
is well-defined. Notice that
\mathtt{cost}_{\gamma}(B)=\mathtt{cost}_{\beta^{\prime}}(B^{\prime})+\mathtt{cost}_{\beta^{\prime\prime}}(B^{\prime\prime})\leq q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}=q^{\prime}+(q-q^{\prime\prime})=q
. Thus,
\gamma
is the required assignment of
B
to the first
j^{\prime}
bins with cost at most
q
.

This completes the proof of Theorem
59
.
∎

4.4.2
An Algorithm for an Annotated Variant of Cluster Modification

To show that that
BCC
and
BCE
admit algorithms that runs in time
4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, we do as follows. We first define an annotated variant of
Cluster Completion
called
Annotated Cluster Modification
(
Anno-CM
), and show that solving
BCC
and
BCE
amounts to solving
2^{o(k)}
many instances of
Anno-CM
, each instance of which can be reduced to an instance of
B-in-B
with
s\leq 2k
balls,
t\leq 2k
bins and budget
W=2k
. Theorem
59
will then yield
4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time algorithms for
BCC
and
BCE
.

Notation and Terminology.

For multisets
A
and
B
, by the set difference
A\setminus B
, we mean the “additive set difference” of
A
and
B
; that is,
A\setminus B\subseteq A
and for each
x\in A
,
\mathtt{mul}(x,A\setminus B)=\max\left\{{0,\mathtt{mul}(x,A)-\mathtt{mul}(x,B)}\right\}
. For example, if
A=\left\{{x,y,y,y,z,z,z}\right\}
and
B=\left\{{x,x,y,w}\right\}
, then
A\setminus B=\left\{{y,y,z,z,z}\right\}
. For a multiset
X
of non-negative integers and
\eta\geq 0
, we say that
X
is
\eta
-balanced if
{|{x-y}|}\leq\eta
for every
x,y\in X
. For a graph
G
, by the set of component sizes of
G
, denoted by
\mathtt{CS}(G)
, we mean the multiset of positive integers where for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
, the multiplicity of
j
in
\mathtt{CS}(G)
is precisely the number of components of
G
of size exactly
j
; that is,
\mathtt{CS}(G)
is the multiset
\left\{{{|{H}|}~|~H\text{ is a connected component of }G}\right\}
. In particular, a graph
G
is
\eta
-balanced if and only if
\mathtt{CS}(G)
is
\eta
-balanced. Consider a cluster graph
G
and an integer
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Recall that for a partition
X^{\prime}=\left\{{x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{s}}\right\}
of
\ell
, we say that
X^{\prime}
is
G
-valid if
G
contains
s
distinct components
H_{1},H_{2},\ldots,H_{s}
such that
{|{H_{i}}|}=x^{\prime}_{i}
for every
i\in[s]
. For partitions
X
and
X^{\prime}
of
\ell
such that
X^{\prime}
is
G
-valid, by a modification of
G
with respect to
(X,X^{\prime})
, we mean a cluster graph
\hat{G}
obtained from
G
by adding edges so that
\mathtt{CS}(\hat{G})=(\mathtt{CS}(G)\setminus X^{\prime})\cup X
; that is, we obtain
\hat{G}
from
G
by merging components so that
{|{X^{\prime}}|}
components of
G
of sizes from
X^{\prime}
are “replaced” by
{|{X}|}
components of sizes from
X
. Notice that a modification of
G
w.r.t.
(X,X^{\prime})
need not exist, and that a modification need not be unique if one exists. Consider a modification
\hat{G}
of
G
w.r.t.
(X,X^{\prime})
(assuming one exists). Notice that we can identify
\hat{G}
with a function that maps
X^{\prime}
to
X
: Each
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
is mapped to
x\in X
if and only if the component of
G
corresponding to
x^{\prime}
is merged with other components to form a component of
\hat{G}
of size exactly
x
, and we call this function a witness for the modification
\hat{G}
and denote it by
\mathtt{wit}_{\hat{G}}
, or simply
\mathtt{wit}
when
\hat{G}
is clear from the context. That is, the witness for
\hat{G}
is the function
{{\mathtt{wit}}:{X^{\prime}}\rightarrow{X}}
, where for each
x\in X
, a component of
\hat{G}
of size
x
replaces exactly
{|{\mathtt{wit}^{-1}(x)}|}
components of
G
of sizes from the multiset
\mathtt{wit}^{-1}(x)
; we thus have
\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathtt{wit}^{-1}(x)}x^{\prime}=x
for every
x\in X
.
\lipicsEnd

Observation 63
.

Consider a cluster graph
G
, partitions
X=\left\{{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}}\right\}
and
X^{\prime}=\left\{{x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{s}}\right\}
of
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
such that
X^{\prime}
is
G
-valid, and a modification
\hat{G}
of
G
w.r.t.
(X,X^{\prime})
(assuming a modification exists).
Corresponding to each
x^{\prime}_{i}\in X^{\prime}
and
x_{j}\in X
such that
\mathtt{wit}(x^{\prime}_{i})=x_{j}
, a component
H
of
G
with
{|{H}|}=x^{\prime}_{i}
is merged with some other components of
G
to form a component
\hat{H}
of
\hat{G}
with
{|{\hat{H}}|}=x_{j}
. To form
\hat{H}
, notice that we need to add exactly
{|{\hat{H}}|}-{|{H}|}=x_{j}-x^{\prime}_{i}
edges incident with each vertex of
H
; in total, we need to add exactly
{|{H}|}({|{\hat{H}}|}-{|{H}|})=x^{\prime}_{i}(x_{j}-x^{\prime}_{i})
edges incident with
V(H)
. Thus,
{|{E(\hat{G})\setminus E(G)}|}
, i.e., the total number of edges we need to add to
G
to form
\hat{G}
is precisely
(1/2)\sum_{i\in[s]}\left({x^{\prime}_{i}\cdot(\mathtt{wit}(x^{\prime}_{i})-x^{\prime}_{i})}\right)
; the
1/2
accounts for the fact that each edge in
E(\hat{G})\setminus E(G)
is counted twice in the sum
\sum_{i\in[s]}\left({x^{\prime}_{i}\cdot(\mathtt{wit}(x^{\prime}_{i})-x^{\prime}_{i})}\right)
.

We now define the following problem.

Input:

A cluster graph, a non-negative integer
k
and two partitions
X
and
X^{\prime}
of an integer
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
such that
X^{\prime}
is
G
-valid.

Task:

Decide if there exists a modification
\hat{G}
of
G
with respect to
(X,X^{\prime})
such that
{|{E(\hat{G})\setminus E(G)}|}\leq k
.

Annotated Cluster Modification (Anno-CM)

Theorem 64
.

Anno-CM
admits an algorithm that runs in time
2^{\ell}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

Proof.

We will show that we can reduce
Anno-CM
to
B-in-B
. The theorem will then follow from Theorem
59
.

Given an instance
(G,k,X,X^{\prime})
of
Anno-CM
, where
X^{\prime}=\left\{{x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{s}}\right\}
and
X=\left\{{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}}\right\}
are partitions of
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we construct an instance of
B-in-B
as follows: We take
s
balls indexed by
1,2,\ldots,s
with sizes
x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{s}
, and
t
bins indexed by
1,2,\ldots,t
with capacities
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}
, a budget
W=2k
and a cost function
{{\mathtt{cost}}:{[s]}\rightarrow{[t]}}
where
\mathtt{cost}(i,j)=x^{\prime}_{i}(x_{j}-x^{\prime}_{i})
if
x_{j}\geq x^{\prime}_{i}
and
\mathtt{cost}(i,j)=2k+1
otherwise.

Notice that we can construct this instance of
B-in-B
in polynomial time. Also, as
X^{\prime}
and
X
are partitions of
\ell
, we have
s={|{X^{\prime}}|}\leq\ell
and
t={|{X}|}\leq\ell
; as
X^{\prime}
is
G
-valid, we also have
\ell\leq n
; finally, we can assume without loss of generality that
k\leq\binom{n}{2}
. As we have
s,t\leq\ell\leq n
and
W=2k\leq 2\binom{n}{2}
, Theorem
59
implies that we can solve this instance of
B-in-B
in time
2^{\ell}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. We return that the given instance of
Anno-CM
is a yes-instance if and only if the constructed instance of
B-in-B
is a yes-instance.

To complete the proof, we only need to show that the two instances are equivalent. This follows from the fact that every modification
\hat{G}
of
G
w.r.t.
(X,X^{\prime})
with
{|{E(\hat{G})\setminus E(G)}|}\leq k
corresponds to an assignment
{{\beta}:{[s]}\rightarrow{[t]}}
with cost at most
W=2k
and vice versa: For every
i\in[s]
and
j\in[t]
, we will have
\beta(i)=j
if and only if
\mathtt{wit}_{\hat{G}}(x^{\prime}_{i})=x_{j}
. As it is straightforward to verify that this correspondence between
\hat{G}
and
\beta
indeed shows the equivalence between the two instances, we omit the technical details.
∎

4.4.3
A Faster Algorithm for BCC

We now prove the following lemma, which shows that solving
BCC
amounts to solving
2^{o(k)}
many instances of
Anno-CM
.

Lemma 65
.

Consider an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
BCC
.
If
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance, then either
G
is
\eta
-balanced or there exist an integer
\ell\in[2k]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\}
and partitions
X
and
X^{\prime}
of
\ell
such that
X^{\prime}
is
G
-valid, the multiset
(\mathtt{CS}(G)\setminus X^{\prime})\cup X
is
\eta
-balanced, and
(G,k,X,X^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of
Anno-CM
.

Proof.

Consider an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
BCC
.
Assume that
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance.
If
G
is
\eta
-balanced, then the lemma trivially holds; recall that
G
is a cluster graph.
So, assume that
G
is not
\eta
-balanced. We will show that
\ell
,
X
and
X^{\prime}
, as required by the statement of lemma, exist by invoking Lemma
52
.

By Lemma
52
, there exist
\ell\in[2k]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\}
and a partition
X=\left\{{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}}\right\}
of
\ell
such that for each
i\in[t]
, there exists a partition
X_{i}
of
x_{i}
with the following properties: (1)
\bigcup_{i\in[t]}X_{i}
is
G
-valid, (2)
\sum_{i\in[t]}\mathtt{spp}(X_{i})\leq k
, and (3) a completion of
G
w.r.t.
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
is
\eta
-balanced.

Let
\hat{G}
be a completion of
G
w.r.t.
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
. Then, by Observation
49
and property (3) above,
\hat{G}
is
\eta
-balanced; by Observation
49
and property (2) above,
{|{E(\hat{G})\setminus E(G)}|}=\sum_{i\in[t]}\mathtt{spp}(X_{i})\leq k
.
We take
X^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in[t]}X_{i}
. Then, by the definition of a modification of
G
w.r.t.
(X,X^{\prime})
,
\hat{G}
is a modification of
G
w.r.t.
(X,X^{\prime})
. And as
{|{E(\hat{G})\leq E(G)}|}\leq k
, we can conclude that
(G,k,X,X^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of
Anno-CM
. Again, as
\hat{G}
is a modification of
G
w.r.t.
(X,X^{\prime})
, we also have
(\mathtt{CS}(G)\setminus X^{\prime})\cup X=\mathtt{CS}(\hat{G})
, which is
\eta
-balanced as
\hat{G}
is an
\eta
-balanced graph. Thus,
\ell,X
and
X^{\prime}
satisfy all the properties required by the statement of the lemma.
∎

We are now ready to describe our algorithm for
BCC
, which we call
Fast-Algo-BCC
.

Fast-Algo-BCC
.

Given an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
BCC
as input, we proceed as follows.
Recall that
G
is a cluster graph.

Step 1.

If
G
is
\eta
-balanced, then we return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance, and terminate.

Step 2.

If
k\leq 0
, then we return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a no-instance, and terminate.

Step 3.

We use the algorithm of Proposition
48
to generate all partitions of
\ell
for all
\ell\in[2k]
.

Step 4.

For each
\ell\in[2k]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\}
, we do as follows.

Step 4.1.

For each pair of partitions
X
and
X^{\prime}
of
\ell
such that
(\mathtt{CS}(G)\setminus X^{\prime})\cup X
is
\eta
-balanced, we do as follows.

Step 4.1.1.

We consider the instance
(G,k,X,X^{\prime})
of
Anno-CM
, and use the algorithm of Theorem
64
to solve this instance. If
(G,k,X,X^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of
Anno-CM
, then we return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCC
, and terminate.

Step 5.

We return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a no-instance, and terminate.

The correctness of
Fast-Algo-BCC
follows from Lemma
65
. To analyse its running time, observe that the most time-consuming step is Step 4.1.1., each execution of which requires time
2^{\ell}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
(by Theorem
64
). Also, by Proposition
48
, the number of partitions of an integer
\ell
is
2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\ell})}
, and hence the number of pairs
(X,X^{\prime})
that we consider in Step 4.1 is
2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\ell})}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\ell})}=2^{o(\ell)}
. Finally, we go over
2k-1
choices of
\ell
in Step 4, and as we only consider
\ell\leq 2k
, we can conclude that the total running time is bounded by
2k\cdot 2^{o(2k)}\cdot 2^{2k}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}=4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
We thus have the following result.

Theorem 66
.

BCC
admits an algorithm that runs in time
4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

4.4.4
A Faster Algorithm for BCE

We now turn to
BCE
. To design an algorithm for
BCE
, notice that as we did earlier, we only need to design an algorithm for
BCE
when the input graph is a cluster graph, as we can branch on induced
P_{3}
s otherwise. Specifically, we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 67
.

BCE
on cluster graphs admits an algorithm that runs in time
4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

Assuming Theorem
67
, let us first prove the following result.

Theorem 68
.

BCE
admits an algorithm that runs in time
4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

Proof Sketch.

The proof is nearly identical to the proof of Theorem
55
.

Given an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
BCE
, our algorithm works as follows. If
G
is a cluster graph, then we use the algorithm of Theorem
67
to solve the problem. Otherwise, we find an induced
P_{3}
, say
uvw
and recursively call our algorithm on the three instances
(G-uv,k-1,\eta),(G-vw,k-1,\eta)
and
(G+uw,k-1,\eta)
.

As for the running time, notice that each time we find a
P_{3}
, we make
3
recursive calls, until
k
becomes
0
or the graph becomes a cluster graph
G
(whichever happens earlier). Hence the total number of recursive calls is at most
\sum_{i=0}^{k}3^{i}
. Now, on any branch of the computation, as we first make
i\in[k]_{0}
edge modifications until the graph becomes a cluster graph and only then invoke the algorithm of Theorem
67
with parameter
k-i
, the latter step will only take time
4^{k-i+o(k-i)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. Thus the total running time is bounded by
\sum_{i=0}^{k}3^{i}\cdot 4^{k-i+o(k-i)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\leq 4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
∎

Now we only have to prove Theorem
67
. Before moving to a formal proof of Theorem
67
, we highlight some of the key ideas first.

Ideas we use in our algorithm for
BCE
on cluster graphs.

For the most part, we use a combination of arguments we already used in our previous algorithm for
BCE
on cluster graphs (
Algo-BCE-C
) and our
4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time algorithm for
BCC
(
Fast-Algo-BCC
). But there are subtle differences. First of all, observe that a cluster graph
G
is completely specified by its component sizes, i.e., the multiset
\mathtt{CS}(G)
; any two cluster graphs
H
and
H^{\prime}
are isomorphic if and only if
\mathtt{CS}(H)=\mathtt{CS}(H^{\prime})
. We will rely on this fact.
Now, suppose
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCE
, where
G
is a cluster graph, and suppose
F\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}
is the solution that we are looking for. Let
F_{1}\subseteq F
be the set of edges that we delete from
G
and
F_{2}=F\setminus F_{1}
be the set of edges that we add to
G
. Then
G-F_{1}
is a cluster graph. Also,
F_{1}
corresponds to a pair of partitions
(Y,Y^{\prime})
of
\ell_{1}={|{V(F_{1})}|}
: each
y\in Y
corresponds to a component of
G
of size
y
that was split into smaller components when we deleted
F_{1}
, and each
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
corresponds to one of the “new” components of
G-F_{1}
of size
y^{\prime}
that was formed when we deleted
F_{1}
. Thus
\mathtt{CS}(G-F_{1})=(\mathtt{CS}(G)\setminus Y)\cup Y^{\prime}
. Once again,
we obtain
G^{\prime}
from
G
by deleting the edges of
F_{1}
, or equivalently, we obtain
G
from
G-F_{1}
by adding the edges of
F_{1}
. In other words,
(G-F_{1},{|{F_{1}}|},Y,Y^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of
Anno-CM
. But then, so is
(G^{\prime},{|{F_{1}}|},Y,Y^{\prime})
for
any cluster
graph
G^{\prime}
with
\mathtt{CS}(G^{\prime})=\mathtt{CS}(G-F_{1})
. The rest of the arguments are identical to those in
Fast-Algo-BCC
. We obtain
G\triangle F
by adding the edges of
F_{2}
to the cluster graph
G-F_{1}
. That is, for appropriate partitions
X
and
X^{\prime}
of
{|{V(F_{2})}|}
,
(G-F_{1},{|{F_{2}}|},X,X^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of
Anno-CM
, and therefore,
(G^{\prime},{|{F_{2}}|},X,X^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of

Anno-CM
.
So in our algorithm, we guess
{|{F_{1}}|},{|{F_{2}}|},{|{V(F_{1})}|},{|{V(F_{2})}|},Y,Y^{\prime}
and
X,X^{\prime}
and we simply check if
(G^{\prime},{|{F_{1}}|},Y,Y^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of
Anno-CM
, and if
(G^{\prime},{|{F_{2}}|},X,X^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of
Anno-CM
, where
G^{\prime}
is an arbitrary cluster graph with
\mathtt{CS}(G^{\prime})=\mathtt{CS}(G)\setminus Y)\cup Y^{\prime}
.
\lipicsEnd

We formalise the above ideas in the following lemma, which will establish the correctness our algorithm for
BCE
on cluster graphs.

Lemma 69
.

Consider an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
BCE
, where
G
is a cluster graph.
If
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance, then one of the following statements holds.

1.

The instance
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCC
.

2.

The instance
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCD
.

3.

There exist integers
k_{1},k_{2},\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
with
k_{1}+k_{2}\leq k
and
\ell_{1}\in[2k_{1}]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\},\ell_{2}\in[2k_{2}]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\}
such that there exist partitions
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
of
\ell_{1}
and
X
and
X^{\prime}
of
\ell_{2}
with the following properties:

(a)

Y
is
G
-valid;

(b)

(G^{\prime},k_{1},Y,Y^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of
Anno-CM
, where
G^{\prime}
is a cluster graph with
\mathtt{CS}(G^{\prime})=(\mathtt{CS}(G)\setminus Y)\cup Y^{\prime}
;

(c)

X^{\prime}
is
G^{\prime}
-valid;

(d)

the multiset
(\mathtt{CS}(G^{\prime})\setminus X^{\prime})\cup X
is
\eta
-balanced;

(e)

(G^{\prime},k_{2},X,X^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of
Anno-CM
.

Proof Sketch.

The proof closely follows the proof of Lemma
57
. We only highlight the most salient arguments. Assume that
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCE
, and let
F
be a solution for
(G,k,\eta)
. Let
F_{1}\subseteq F
be the set of edges that we delete from
G
and
F_{2}=F\setminus F_{1}
be the set of edges that we add to
G
. Then
G^{\prime}=G-F_{1}
is a cluster graph.

We take
k_{i}={|{F_{i}}|}
and
\ell_{i}={|{V(F_{i})}|}
for each
i\in[2]
. We associate
F_{1}
with a pair of partitions
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
of
\ell_{1}
; the partition
Y
corresponds to the components of
G
that are split into smaller components when we delete
F_{1}
, and the partition
Y^{\prime}
corresponds to the “new” components of
G^{\prime}=G-F_{1}
that are formed when we delete
F_{1}
. In other words, we obtain
G
from
G^{\prime}
by adding edges, and in particular,
G
is a modification of
G^{\prime}
w.r.t.
(Y,Y^{\prime})
. As
E(G)\setminus E(G^{\prime})=F_{1}
and
{|{F_{1}}|}=k_{1}
,
(G^{\prime},k_{1},Y,Y^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of
Anno-CM
. We associate
F_{2}
with a pair of partitions
X
and
X^{\prime}
of
\ell_{2}
. The partition
X^{\prime}
corresponds to the components of
G^{\prime}
that are merged together to form the components of
G^{\prime}+F_{2}=G\triangle F
; the partition
X
corresponds to the “new” components of
G^{\prime}+F_{2}
that are formed by merging together components of
G^{\prime}
. In other words,
G^{\prime}+F_{2}=G\triangle F
is a modification of
G
w.r.t.
(X,X^{\prime})
, and thus
\mathtt{CS}(G\triangle F)=(\mathtt{CS}(G^{\prime}+F_{2})\setminus X^{\prime})\cup X
. As
E(G\triangle F)\setminus E(G^{\prime})=F_{2}
and
{|{F_{2}}|}=k_{2}
,
(G^{\prime},k_{2},X,X^{\prime})
is a yes-instance of
Anno-CM
, and as the graph
G\triangle F
is
\eta
-balanced, the multiset
\mathtt{CS}(G\triangle F)=(\mathtt{CS}(G^{\prime}+F_{2})\setminus X^{\prime})\cup X
is
\eta
-balanced.
∎

Based on Lemma
69
, we design an algorithm for
BCE
on cluster graphs, which we call
Fast-Algo-BCE-C
.

Fast-Algo-BCE-C
.

Given an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
BCE
as input, where
G
is a cluster graph, we proceed as follows.

Step 1.

If
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCC
or a yes-instance of
BCD
, we return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCE
, and terminate. To do this, we use the algorithms of Theorems
66
and
42
.

Step 2.

If
k\leq 0
, then we return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a no-instance, and terminate.

Step 3.

We use the algorithm of Proposition
48
to generate all partitions of
\ell
for all
\ell\in[2k]
.

Step 4.

For every choice of
k_{1},k_{2}\in[k]
with
k_{1}+k_{2}\leq k
and
\ell_{1}\in[2k_{1}]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\},\ell_{2}\in[2k_{2}]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\}
, we do as follows.

Step 4.1.

For each pair of partitions
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
of
\ell_{1}
and each pair of partitions
X
and
X^{\prime}
of
\ell_{2}
, such that
Y
is
G
-valid, we do as follows.

Step 4.1.1.

We construct a cluster graph
G^{\prime}
with
\mathtt{CS}(G^{\prime})=(\mathtt{CS}(G)\setminus Y)\cup Y^{\prime}
.

Step 4.1.2

If
X^{\prime}
is
G^{\prime}
-valid, the multiset
(\mathtt{CS}(G^{\prime})\setminus X^{\prime})\cup X
is
\eta
-balanced, and both
(G^{\prime},k_{1},Y,Y^{\prime})
and
(G^{\prime},k_{2},X,X^{\prime})
are yes-instances of
Anno-CM
, then we return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCE
, and terminate. To check if
(G^{\prime},k_{1},Y,Y^{\prime})
and
(G^{\prime},k_{2},X,X^{\prime})
are yes-instances of
Anno-CM
, we use the algorithm of Theorem
64
.

Step 5.

We return that
(G,k,\eta)
is a no-instance, and terminate.

The correctness of the algorithm follows from Lemma
69
. We now analyse the running time.

Lemma 70
.

Fast-Algo-BCE-C
runs in time
4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

Proof.

Observe that Steps 2 and 5 take only polynomial time.
By Theorems
66
and
42
, Step 1 takes time at most
4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. By Proposition
48
, Step 3 takes time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}=2^{o(k)}
.
Now, in Step 4, we need to go over at most
k+1
choices of
k_{i}
and at most
2k_{i}-1
choices of
\ell_{i}
for each
i\in[2]
; and we can assume without loss of generality that
k\leq\binom{n}{2}
.
That is, there are only
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
choices for the tuple
(k_{1},k_{2},\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
. In Step 4.1, we go over all possible choices of
(Y,Y^{\prime},X,X^{\prime})
. As
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
are partitions of
\ell_{1}\leq 2k_{1}\leq 2k
, by Proposition
48
, each of them has at most
p(2k)=2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
choices; similarly, each of
X
and
X^{\prime}
also has at most
p(2k)=2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}
choices. Thus, there are
2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}=2^{\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{k})}=2^{o(k)}
choices for the tuple
(Y,Y^{\prime},X,X^{\prime})
.
Finally, in Step 4.1.2, for each choice of
(Y,Y^{\prime},X,X^{\prime})
, we invoke the algorithm of Theorem
64
twice; on the instance
(G^{\prime},k_{1},Y,Y^{\prime})
, the algorithm takes time
2^{\ell_{1}}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\leq 2^{2k_{1}}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}=4^{k_{1}}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
; similarly, on the instance
(G^{\prime},k_{1},Y,Y^{\prime})
, it takes time at most
4^{k_{2}}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. Thus each execution of Step 4.1.2 takes time
4^{k_{1}}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}+4^{k_{2}}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}\leq 4^{k_{1}+k_{2}}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}=4^{k}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. Therefore, the overall running time of the algorithm is bounded by
4^{k+o(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
∎

This completes the proof of Theorem
67
.

5
FPT Algorithms for the Counting Versions of BCC, BCD and BCE

In this section, we show that the
counting
versions of
BCC
,
BCD
, and
BCE
admit
FPT
algorithms. We show that same ideas that we used in Section
4.3
can be adapted to work for the counting versions of the problems as well. In particular, the idea of associating solutions with sets of integer partitions adapt nicely to the counting setting—we can enumerate these sets of partitions and count the number of solutions that correspond to each set of partitions. We formally define the counting version of
BCC
as follows.

Input:

A cluster graph
G
and non-negative integers
k
and
\eta
.

Task:

Output the number of sets
F\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}\setminus E(G)
such that
{|{F}|}\leq k
and
G+F
is an
\eta
-balanced cluster graph.

#Balanced Cluster Completion (#BCC)

We define the counting versions of
BCD
and
BCE
analogously. We show that
#BCC
can be solved in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, and
#BCD
and
#BCE
can be solved in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

Notation.

Recall the following notation. For a multiset
X
and
x\in X
,
\mathtt{mul}(x,X)
denotes the multiplicity of
x
in
X
. And
\mathtt{US}(X)
denotes the underlying set of
X
. We say that a multiset
X
of non-negative integers is
\eta
-balanced if
{|{x-y}|}\leq\eta
for every
x,y\in X
.
For a graph
G
,
\mathtt{CC}(G)
denotes the set of connected components of
G
, and
\mathtt{CS}(G)
denotes the multiset of the component sizes of
G
. In this section, whenever the graph
G
is clear from the context, we use
\beta(j)
to denote the number of connected components of
G
of size exactly
j
, i.e.,
\beta(j)=\mathtt{mul}(j,\mathtt{CS}(G))
. For a decision problem
\Pi
and its natural counting version
\#\Pi
, we use
{\#\mathtt{sol}}_{\Pi}(I)
to denote the number of solutions for
\Pi
on the input instance
I
; for example,
{\#\mathtt{sol}}_{BCC}(G,k,\eta)
. And whenever the problem is clear from the context, we may drop the subscript and simply write
{\#\mathtt{sol}}(I)
.

5.1
FPT Algorithm for (a Colour-Restricted Variant of) #BCC

We begin with
#BCC
. But instead of solving
#BCC
directly, we solve a more general version of the problem in which the each component of the input graph has one of
q+1
colours
0,1,\ldots,q
, and certain colour-based constraints disallow adding edges between certain components. This colour-restricted version will be useful in designing our algorithm for
#BCE
as well.

We now formally introduce the colour-restricted variant of
BCC
.
Consider a graph
G
. Recall that
\mathtt{CC}(G)
denotes the set of connected components of
G
.
Consider a colouring function
{{\mu}:{\mathtt{CC}(G)}\rightarrow{[q]_{0}}}
that assigns colours to the connected components of
G
. Notice that
\mu
assigns colours to the components of
G
and not to individual vertices. But with a slight abuse of notation, for a vertex
v
, we define
\mu(v)=\mu(H)
, where
H
is the connected component of
G
that contains
v
.
Consider
G
and
{{\mu}:{\mathtt{CC}(G)}\rightarrow{[q]_{0}}}
. For
F\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}
, we say that
F
is
colour-restricted
if for every
uv\in F
, either
\mu(u)=\mu(v)=0
or
\mu(u)\neq\mu(v)
. That is,
F
is colour-restricted if and only if for every
uv\in F
, either both
u
and
v
have colour
0
, or
u
and
v
have different colours.

Input:

A cluster graph
G
along with a colouring function
{{\mu}:{\mathtt{CC}(G)}\rightarrow{[q]_{0}}}
, and a non-negative integer
k

Task:

Decide if there exists
F\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}\setminus E(G)
such that
F
is colour-restricted,
{|{F}|}\leq k
and
G+F
is an
\eta
-balanced cluster graph.

Colour-Restricted Balanced Cluster Completion (CRBCC)

Observe that
CRBCC
is identical to
BCC
, except that the solution has to be colour-restricted. In particular,
BCC
is a special case of
CRBCC
in which all components are of colour
0
. Recall that to solve
BCC
(and its counting version
#BCC
), we relied on the fact that each solution
F
for
BCC
could naturally be associated with a multiset of integer partitions in which each partition describes the sizes of the components of
G
that are merged together to form a component of
G+F
. But this is not quite the case with
CRBCC
, as the colours of the components have to be taken into account. Nonetheless, notice that we can still associate each solution
F
for
CRBCC
with multisets of (size, colour) pairs that describe the size and colour of individual components of
G
that are merged together to form a component of
G+F
. That is, each solution
F
for
CRBCC
can be associated with a pair
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
, where
X=\left\{{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}}\right\}
is a partition of
{|{V(F)}|}
and for every
i\in[t]
,
X_{i}=\left\{{(x_{i1},\tau_{i1}),(x_{i2},\tau_{i2}),\ldots,(x_{ir_{i}},\tau_{ir_{i}})}\right\}
is such that
\left\{{x_{11},x_{12},\ldots,x_{ir_{i}}}\right\}
is a partition of
x_{i}
, and
\tau_{ij}\in[q]_{0}
; for each
i\in[t]
,
x_{i1},x_{i2},\ldots,x_{ir_{i}}
are the sizes of the
r_{i}
components of
G
that are merged together by
F
to form a component of
G+F
of size
x_{i}
, and the corresponding
\tau_{ij}
s are the colours of those
r_{i}
components. We extend the earlier definitions of various terms involving integer partitions—for example, definitions of a partition
X
being
G
-valid, a completion of a cluster graph w.r.t. a pair
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
etc.—to include the colours. Similarly, by suitably adapting the proof of Lemma
51
, we derive the following result that bounds the number of choices for the pair
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
.

Lemma 71
.

Consider positive integers
\ell
and
q
. The number of choices for the pair
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
, where
X=\left\{{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}}\right\}
is a partition of
\ell
, and for every
i\in[t]
,
X_{i}=\left\{{(x_{i1},\tau_{i1}),(x_{i2},\tau_{i2}),\ldots,(x_{ir_{i}},\tau_{ir_{i}})}\right\}
is such that
\left\{{x_{11},x_{12},\ldots,x_{ir_{i}}}\right\}
is a partition of
x_{i}
, and
\tau_{ij}\in[q]_{0}
for every
j\in[r_{i}]
, is
2^{\mathcal{O}(\ell\log q)}
.

Proof.

Recall that for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
p(\ell)
denotes the number of partitions of
\ell
.
Recall also that by Proposition
48
, there exists a constant
C
such that
p(\ell)\leq 2^{C\sqrt{\ell}}
for every
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Among all the partitions of
\ell
, let
X^{*}=\left\{{x^{*}_{1},x^{*}_{2},\ldots,x^{*}_{t^{*}}}\right\}
be a partition that maximises the product
p(x_{1})(q+1)^{x_{1}}\cdot p(x_{2})(q+1)^{x_{2}}\cdots p(x_{t^{*}})(q+1)^{x_{t^{*}}}
.

Now, for each fixed partition
x_{i1}+x_{i2}+\cdots+x_{ir_{i}}
of
x_{i}
, notice that the number of choices for the multiset
X_{i}=\left\{{(x_{i1},\tau_{i1}),(x_{i2},\tau_{i2}),\ldots,(x_{ir_{i}},\tau_{ir_{i}})}\right\}
, where
\tau_{ij}\in[q]_{0}
, is precisely
(q+1)^{r_{i}}\leq(q+1)^{x_{i}}
; this is true because each
\tau_{ij}
has
q+1
choices, and as
\left\{{x_{i1},x_{i2},\ldots,x_{ir_{i}}}\right\}
is a partition of
x_{i}
, we have
r_{i}\leq x_{i}
. Hence for each
x_{i}
, the number of choices for the multiset
X_{i}
is at most
p(x_{i})(q+1)^{x_{i}}
. Therefore, for each partition
X
of
\ell
, where
X=\left\{{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}}\right\}
, there are at most
p(x_{1})(q+1)^{x_{1}}\cdot p(x_{2})(q+1)^{x_{2}}\cdots p(x_{t})(q+1)^{x_{t}}
choices for the multiset
\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}
. Finally, by summing over all partitions of
\ell
, we get that the number of choices for
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
is at most

\displaystyle\sum_{X}\prod_{i\in[t]}p(x_{i})(q+1)^{x_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{X}\prod_{j\in[t^{*}]}p(x^{*}_{j})(q+1)^{x^{*}_{j}}

\displaystyle=p(\ell)\prod_{j\in[t^{*}]}p(x^{*}_{j})(q+1)^{x^{*}_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{C\sqrt{\ell}}\prod_{j\in[t^{*}]}2^{C\sqrt{x^{*}_{j}}}(q+1)^{x^{*}_{j}}

\displaystyle=2^{C(\sqrt{\ell}+\sqrt{x^{*}_{1}}+\sqrt{x^{*}_{2}}+\cdots+\sqrt{x^{*}_{t^{*}}})}\cdot(q+1)^{x^{*}_{1}+x^{*}_{2}+\cdots x^{*}_{t^{*}}}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{C(\ell+x^{*}_{1}+x^{*}_{2}+\cdots x^{*}_{t^{*}})}\cdot(q+1)^{x^{*}_{1}+x^{*}_{2}+\cdots x^{*}_{t^{*}}}

\displaystyle=2^{\mathcal{O}(\ell)}\cdot(q+1)^{\ell}.

∎

Consider a graph
G
and
{{\mu}:{\mathtt{CC}(G)}\rightarrow{[q]_{0}}}
. For
j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tau\in[q]_{0}
, let
\beta(j,\tau)
be the number of components of
G
of size
j
and colour
\tau
. Also, let
\#\mathtt{com}(G,\mu,(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}))
be the number of completions of
G
w.r.t.
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
, which we compute next.

Lemma 72
.

Consider a cluster graph
G
, a colouring function
{{\mu}:{\mathtt{CC}(G)}\rightarrow{[q]_{0}}}
and a positive integer
\ell
. Let
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
be as follows: (a)
X=\left\{{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{t}}\right\}
is a partition of
\ell
, (b) for every
i\in[t]
,
X_{i}=\left\{{(x_{i1},\tau_{i1}),(x_{i2},\tau_{i2}),\ldots,(x_{ir_{i}},\tau_{ir_{i}})}\right\}
is such that
\left\{{x_{11},x_{12},\ldots,x_{ir_{i}}}\right\}
is a partition of
x_{i}
, and
\tau_{ij}\in[q]_{0}
for every
j\in[r_{i}]
, and (c)
X^{\prime}
is
G
-valid, where
X^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\in[t]}X_{i}
. For
i\in[t],j\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tau\in[q]_{0}
, let
d(i,j,\tau)
be defined as follows:
d(1,j,\tau)=0
and
d(i,j,\tau)=\sum_{s=1}^{i-1}\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{s})
for
i>1
. For
\hat{X}\in\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}
, let
m(\hat{X})
denote the multiplicity of
\hat{X}
in
\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}
.
Then,

\#\mathtt{com}(G,\mu,(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}))=\frac{\prod_{i\in[t]}\prod_{(j,\tau)\in\mathtt{US}(X_{i})}\binom{\beta(j,\tau)-d(i,j,\tau)}{\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{i})}}{\prod_{\hat{X}\in\mathtt{US}(\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})}m(\hat{X})!}.

Moreover, given
G,\mu
and
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
, we can compute
\#\mathtt{com}(G,\mu,(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}))
in polynomial time.

Proof.

Observe that
\#\mathtt{com}(G,\mu,(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}))
is precisely the number of ways in which we can choose
t
pairwise-disjoint sets
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{t}
of connected components of
G
such that for each
i\in[t]
,
\mathcal{D}_{i}
consists of
r_{i}
distinct components
H_{i1},H_{i2},\ldots,H_{ir_{i}}
with
{|{H_{ij}}|}=x_{ij}
and
\mu(H_{ij})=\tau_{ij}
for every
j\in[r_{i}]
. For
i=1
to
t
in this order, let us count the number of ways in which we can choose
\mathcal{D}_{i}
. Observe that for each
(j,\tau)\in\mathtt{US}(X_{1})
,
\mathcal{D}_{1}
consists of
\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{1})
components of size
j
and colour
\tau
. And
G
has
\beta(j,\tau)
components of
G
of size
j
and colour
\tau
. Therefore, we can choose
\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{1})
many such components in
\binom{\beta(j,\tau)}{\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{1})}
ways, and hence the number of choices for
\mathcal{D}_{1}
is
\prod_{(j,\tau)\in\mathtt{US}(X_{1})}\binom{\beta(j,\tau)}{\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{1})}
. Now, consider
i>1
and
(j,\tau)\in\mathtt{US}(X_{i})
. Out of the
\beta(j,\tau)
components of size
j
and colour
\tau
, for each
s<i
, we choose exactly
\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{s})
components; so when we come to
i
, we will have already chosen
\sum_{s=1}^{i-1}\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{s})=d(i,j,\tau)
such components, and we will be left with
\beta(j,\tau)-d(i,j,\tau)
components. Hence we can choose
\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{i})
of those components in
\binom{\beta(j,\tau)-d(i,j,\tau)}{\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{i})}
ways, which implies that the number of choices for
\mathcal{D}_{i}
is precisely
\prod_{(j,\tau)\in\mathtt{US}(X_{i})}\binom{\beta(j,\tau)-d(i,j,\tau)}{\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{i})}
. Thus the number of choices for the ordered sequence
(\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{t})
is
\prod_{i\in[t]}\prod_{(j,\tau)\in\mathtt{US}(X_{i})}\binom{\beta(j,\tau)-d(i,j,\tau)}{\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{i})}
.

Now, notice that the order of the sets
\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{t}
is immaterial. And for each
\hat{X}\in\mathtt{US}(\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
,
\left\{{\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{t}}\right\}
contains
m_{\hat{X}}
identical sets; we think of
\mathcal{D}_{i}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i^{\prime}}
as identical if
X_{i}=X_{i^{\prime}}
. Any permutation of these
m_{\hat{X}}
sets would all lead to the same set
\left\{{\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{t}}\right\}
, and there are
m_{\hat{X}}!
such permutations. Thus, to obtain the number of choices for
\left\{{\mathcal{D}_{1},\mathcal{D}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{D}_{t}}\right\}
, we must divide the product
\prod_{i\in[t]}\prod_{(j,\tau)\in\mathtt{US}(X_{i})}\binom{\beta(j,\tau)-d(i,j,\tau)}{\mathtt{mul}((j,\tau),X_{i})}
by
\prod_{\hat{X}\in\mathtt{US}(\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})}m_{\hat{X}}!
. Finally, as all the numbers involved in both these products can be encoded using
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
bits, we can compute
\#\mathtt{com}(G,\mu,(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}))
in polynomial time.
∎

Consider an instance
(G,\mu,k,\eta)
of
CRBCC
. To solve
CRBCC
and its counting variant
#CRBCC
, we can use the correspondence between solutions for
(G,\mu,k,\eta)
and completions of
G
w.r.t. pairs
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
with appropriate properties. Among other properties, we would in particular need the
X_{i}
s to be colour-restricted; for
X_{i}=\left\{{(x_{i1},\tau_{i1}),(x_{i2},\tau_{i2}),\ldots,(x_{ir_{i}},\tau_{ir_{i}})}\right\}
, where each
\tau_{ij}\in[q]_{0}
, we say that
X_{i}
is colour-restricted if for each
p\in[q]
, there exists at most one index
j\in[r_{i}]
such that
\tau_{ij}=p
. Notice that if
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}
are colour-restricted, then
F
is colour-restricted for any
F\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}\setminus E(G)
such that
G+F
is a completion of
G
w.r.t.
(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\})
.
Now, as in the case of
BCC
, given an instance
(G,\mu,k,\eta)
of
CRBCC
, by going over all possible choices for
(X,\{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}\})
and summing up
\#\mathtt{com}(G,\mu,(X,\left\{{X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}}\right\}))
we can count the number of solutions for the instance
(G,\mu,k,\eta)
. Thus, Lemmas
71
and
72
together imply the following result.

Lemma 73
.

#CRBCC
admits an algorithm that runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}(q+1)^{\mathcal{O}(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

As
BCC
is a special case of
CRBCC
with just one colour (i.e.,
q+1=1
), Lemma
73
immediately implies the following result.

Theorem 74
.

#BCC
admits an algorithm that runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

5.2
FPT Algorithms for #BCD and #BCE

We now turn to the counting versions of
BCD
and
BCE
, and design algorithms for these problems that run in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. These algorithms, as in the case of our algorithm for
#BCC
, work by enumerating all representative solutions and counting the number of solutions that correspond to each representative. In particular, we reduce the problem to
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}
many instances of appropriately defined variants of
#BCC
,
#BCD
or
#BCE
, where the input graphs are cluster graphs. And then we can easily associate the representative solutions with a set of integer partitions, just as we did in our algorithms for (the decision versions of)
BCC
and
BCE
.

Notation.

Consider a multiset
Y=\left\{{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{s}}\right\}
of positive integers. By
\mathtt{sum}(Y)
, we denote the quantity
\sum_{i\in[s]}y_{j}
. In particular, if
Y
is a partition of
\ell
for some positive integer
\ell
, then
\mathtt{sum}(Y)=\ell
. For a clique
G
on
y_{1}
vertices and a partition
Y_{1}
of
y_{1}
, let
\#\mathtt{del}(y_{1},Y_{1})
denote the number of deletions of
G
with respect to
(\left\{{y_{1}}\right\},Y_{1})
. For a cluster graph
G
, a partition
Y=\left\{{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{s}}\right\}
(of some positive integer
\ell
) such that
Y
is
G
-valid, and a partition
Y_{i}
of
y_{i}
for each
i\in[s]
, let
\#\mathtt{del}(G,(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\}))
denote the number of deletions of
G
with respect to
(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
.
\lipicsEnd

We begin by proving the following lemma, which counts the number of deletions of a clique with respect to an integer partition.

Lemma 75
.

Let
G
be a clique on
y_{1}
vertices, and let
Y_{1}=\left\{{y_{11},y_{12},\ldots,y_{1q_{1}}}\right\}
be a partition of
y_{1}
. For
i\in[q_{1}]
, let
b_{i}
be defined as follows:
b_{1}=0
and for
i>1
,
b_{i}=\sum_{t=1}^{i-1}y_{1t}
. For
y\in\mathtt{US}(Y_{1})
, let
m(y)=\mathtt{mul}(y,Y_{1})
. Then,

\#\mathtt{del}(\left\{{y_{1}}\right\},Y_{1})=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{q_{1}}\binom{y_{1}-b_{i}}{y_{1i}}}{\prod_{y\in\mathtt{US}(Y_{1})}m(y)!}.

Proof.

Observe that
\#\mathtt{del}(\left\{{y_{1}}\right\},Y_{1})
is precisely the number of ways in which we can partition
V(G)
into exactly
q_{1}
parts, say
D_{1},D_{2},\ldots,D_{q_{1}}
such that
{|{D_{i}}|}=y_{1i}
for every
i\in[q_{1}]
. Now, the number of choices for
D_{1}
is precisely the number of ways in which we can choose
y_{11}
vertices from
y_{1}
vertices, which is precisely
\binom{y_{1}}{y_{11}}
. Having chosen
D_{1}
, the number of ways in which we can choose
D_{2}
is precisely the number of ways in which we can choose
y_{12}
vertices from
y_{1}-y_{11}=y_{1}-b_{2}
vertices, which is precisely
\binom{y_{1}-b_{2}}{y_{12}}
. More generally, having chosen
D_{1},D_{2},\ldots,D_{i-1}
, the number of choices for
D_{i}
is precisely the number of ways in which we can choose
y_{1i}
vertices from
y_{1}-\sum_{t=1}^{i-1}{y_{1t}}=y_{1}-b_{i}
vertices, which is precisely
\binom{y_{1}-b_{i}}{y_{1i}}
. Therefore, the number of choices for the tuple
(D_{1},D_{2},\ldots,D_{q_{1}})
is
\prod_{i=1}^{q_{1}}\binom{y_{1}-b_{i}}{y_{1i}}
. Now, as
\left\{{D_{1},D_{2},\ldots,D_{q_{1}}}\right\}
is a partition of
V(G)
, the order of the sets in the partition is immaterial. For each
y\in\mathtt{US}(Y_{1})
, the partition
\left\{{D_{1},D_{2},\ldots,D_{q_{1}}}\right\}
contains exactly
\mathtt{mul}(y,Y_{1})=m(y)
sets of size
y
; any permutation of these
m(y)
sets would all lead to the same partition of
V(G)
, and there are
m(y)!
such permutations. Thus, to obtain,
\#\mathtt{del}(\left\{{y_{1}}\right\},Y_{1})
, we must divide the product
\prod_{i=1}^{q_{1}}\binom{y_{1}-b_{i}}{y_{1i}}
by
\prod_{y\in\mathtt{US}(Y_{1})}m(y)!
, which proves the lemma.
∎

We now extend Lemma
75
to count the number of deletions of a cluster graph w.r.t. a multiset of integer-partitions.

Lemma 76
.

Consider a cluster graph
G
, a positive integer
\ell
, and a pair
(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
, where
Y=\left\{{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{s}}\right\}
is a partition of
\ell
, and
Y_{i}
is a partition of
y_{i}
for each
i\in[s]
, and
Y
is
G
-valid.

1.

Let
\mathtt{US}(\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\}=\left\{{Y^{\prime}_{1},Y^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r}}\right\}
.

2.

For
j\in[r]
, let
m(Y^{\prime}_{j})=\mathtt{mul}(Y^{\prime}_{j},\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
, and
y^{\prime}_{j}=\mathtt{sum}(Y^{\prime}_{j})
.

3.

Consider the
r
integers
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{r}
defined as follows:
c_{1}=0
, and for
j>1
,
c_{j}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[j-1]\\
y^{\prime}_{i}=y^{\prime}_{j}\end{subarray}}m(Y^{\prime}_{i})
.

Then,

\#\mathtt{del}(G,(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\}))=\left({\prod_{j\in[r]}\binom{\beta(y^{\prime}_{j})-c_{j}}{m(j)}}\right)\left({\prod_{i\in[s]}\#\mathtt{del}(\left\{{y_{i}}\right\},Y_{i})}\right).

Proof.

To prove the lemma, observe that
\#\mathtt{del}(G,(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\}))
is precisely product
\zeta_{1}\cdot\zeta_{2}
, where
\zeta_{1}
is the number of choices for
s
components
H_{1},H_{2},\ldots,H_{s}
such that each
H_{i}
corresponds to the partition
Y_{i}
, and
\zeta_{2}
is the number of ways in which the
s
components can be split w.r.t. to the partitions
Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}
. Observe that
\zeta_{2}
is precisely the product of the number of deletions of each
H_{i}
w.r.t.
(\left\{{y_{i}}\right\},Y_{i})
, i.e.,
\zeta_{2}=\prod_{i\in[s]}\#\mathtt{del}(\left\{{y_{i}}\right\},Y_{i})
.

We now calculate
\zeta_{1}
, which is the number of choices for the components
H_{1},H_{2},\ldots,H_{s}
. We ought to be careful while counting the number of choices, as
we cannot treat two components
H_{i}
and
H_{i^{\prime}}
as interchangeable simply because
{|{H_{i}}|}={|{H_{i^{\prime}}}|}
; we also need to consider the corresponding partitions
Y_{i}
and
Y_{i^{\prime}}
that respectively determine how
H_{i}
and
H_{i^{\prime}}
will be split into smaller components. On the other hand, if the partitions
Y_{i}
and
Y_{i^{\prime}}
are identical, then
H_{i}
and
H_{i^{\prime}}
are indeed interchangeable.
Thus, for each
Y^{\prime}_{j}\in\left\{{Y^{\prime}_{1},Y^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,Y^{\prime}_{r}}\right\}=\mathtt{US}(\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
, we need to count the number of ways in which we can choose
\mathtt{mul}(Y^{\prime}_{j},\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})=m(Y^{\prime}_{j})
components of size
y^{\prime}_{j}=\mathtt{sum}(Y^{\prime}_{j})
. Now, notice that for distinct
j,j^{\prime}\in[r]
, we have
Y^{\prime}_{j}\neq Y^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}
, but we may still have
y^{\prime}_{j}=\mathtt{sum}(Y^{\prime}_{j})=\mathtt{sum}(Y^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})=y^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}
. So we will have to account for this possibility as well.

For each
j=1
to
r
in this order, we count the number of ways in which we can choose
m(Y^{\prime}_{j})
components of size
y^{\prime}_{j}
. Notice that for
j=1
, there are
\binom{\beta(y^{\prime}_{j})}{m(Y^{\prime}_{j})}
choices for the required components. Consider
j>1
. Out of the
\beta(y^{\prime}_{j})
components of
G
of size
y^{\prime}_{j}
, for each
i<j
such that
y^{\prime}_{i}=y^{\prime}_{j}
, we have already chosen exactly
m(Y^{\prime}_{i})
components; so when we come to
j
, we will have already chosen
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in[j-1]\\
y^{\prime}_{i}=y^{\prime}_{j}\end{subarray}}m(Y^{\prime}_{i})=c_{j}
components and we will be left with
\beta(y^{\prime}_{j})-c_{j}
components of size
y^{\prime}_{j}
. Hence we can choose
m(Y^{\prime}_{j})
of those components in
\binom{\beta(y^{\prime}_{j})-c_{j}}{m(Y^{\prime}_{j})}
ways. Thus the total number of choices for the
s
components is precisely
\prod_{j\in[r]}\binom{\beta(y^{\prime}_{j})-c_{j}}{m(Y^{\prime}_{j})}
.
∎

An algorithm for #BCD

To design our algorithm for
#BCD
, we rely on some of the arguments that we used in our kernel for
BCD
to bound the number of vertices that belong to the non-clique components. Specifically, we rely on the fact that the main reduction rule that we used to bound the non-clique part of the graph (Reduction Rule
10
) does not change the number of solutions, and the other rules (Reduction Rules
8
and
9
) simply identify no-instances. By exhaustively applying these three rules, we can derive the following result.

Lemma 77
.

There is an algorithm that given an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
#BCD
, runs in polynomial time, and either correctly reports that
(G,k,\eta)
is a no-instance of
BCD
, or returns an instance
(G^{\prime},k^{\prime},\eta)
of
#BCD
and a set
Z\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
such that
{\#\mathtt{sol}}(G,k,\eta)={\#\mathtt{sol}}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime},\eta)
,
{|{Z}|}=\mathcal{O}(k^{4})
,
G^{\prime}-Z
is a cluster graph, and there is no edge between
Z
and
V(G^{\prime})\setminus Z
(that is,
Z
contains all the non-clique components of
G^{\prime}
).

Consider an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
#BCD
. From now on, we assume that we have run the algorithm of Lemma
77
on our input instance and found the set
Z
; let us denote the resulting instance by
(G,k,\eta)
as well. Now, to find
{\#\mathtt{sol}}(G,k,\eta)
, we guess how each solution
F\subseteq E(G)
intersects with
Z
. Notice that each edge of
G
is either an edge of
G[Z]
or an edge of
G-Z
, and hence each solution
F\subseteq E(G)
for
(G,k,\eta)
is the disjoint union of
F_{0}=F\cap E(G[Z])
and
F_{1}=F\cap E(G-Z)
. Thus the components of
G-F
are precisely the components of
G[Z]-F_{0}
and the components of
(G-Z)-F_{1}
. That is,
\mathtt{CC}(G-F)=\mathtt{CC}(G[Z]-F_{0})\cup\mathtt{CC}((G-Z)-F_{1})
, and hence
\mathtt{CS}(G-F)=\mathtt{CS}(G[Z]-F_{0})\cup\mathtt{CS}((G-Z)-F_{1})
; as
G-F
is
\eta
-balanced, we can conclude that
\mathtt{CS}(G[Z]-F_{0})\cup\mathtt{CS}((G-Z)-F_{1})
is
\eta
-balanced. Finally, the facts that
G-F
is an
\eta
-balanced cluster graph and
F_{0}\cap F_{1}=\emptyset
together imply that
G[Z]-F_{0}
and
(G-Z)-F_{1}
are
\eta
-balanced cluster graphs. In particular,
F_{1}
is a solution for the instance
(G-Z,k-{|{F_{0}}|},\eta)
of
BCD
. And recall that by Lemma
77
,
G-Z
is a cluster graph, and therefore, we can associate
F_{1}
with a pair
(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
, where
Y
is a partition of
{|{V(F_{1})}|}
. And by going over all possible
(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
, we can count the number of solutions. We formalise this below.

Formally, for
F_{0}\subseteq E(G[Z])
and a pair
(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
, we say that the pair
(F_{0},(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\}))
is good for
(G,k,\eta)
if the following conditions hold:

•

F_{0}
is a solution for the instance
(G[Z],k,\eta)
of
BCD
;

•

k-{|{F_{0}}|}\geq 1
;

•

(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
is such that
Y=\left\{{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{s}}\right\}
is a partition of
\ell
, for some
\ell\in[2(k-{|{F_{0}}|})]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\}
, and
Y_{i}
is a partition of
y_{i}
for every
i\in[s]
;

•

Y
is
(G-Z)
-valid;

•

\mathtt{CS}(G[Z]-F_{0})\cup((\mathtt{CS}(G-Z)\setminus Y)\cup\hat{Y})
is
\eta
-balanced,
where
\hat{Y}=\bigcup_{i\in[s]}Y_{i}
;

•

\sum_{i\in[s]}\mathtt{spp}(Y_{i})\leq k-{|{F_{0}}|}
.

Notice that for each
F_{0}
and
(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
, we can check in polynomial time if
(F_{0},Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
is good for
(G,k,\eta)
. Notice also that each good
(F_{0},Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
only accounts for solutions of the form
F=F_{0}\cup F_{1}
, where
F_{1}\neq\emptyset
. We also need to account for the number of solutions that are fully contained in
E(G[Z])
. Formally, let
f(G,k,\eta,Z)
be the number of sets
F_{0}\subseteq E(G[Z])
such that
F_{0}
is a solution for the instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
BCD
.

Putting all this together, we have

{\#\mathtt{sol}}(G,k,\eta)=f(G,k,\eta,Z)+\sum_{(F_{0},Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})}\#\mathtt{del}(G-Z,(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\}),

(
Eq. BCD
)

where the summation is over all good
(F_{0},Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
.

The algorithm for
#BCD
is now straightforward. We compute
{\#\mathtt{sol}}(G,k,\eta)
using Equation (
Eq. BCD
). To do this, we first compute
f(G,k,\eta,Z)
by going over all subsets
F_{0}\subseteq E(G[Z])
of size at most
k
, and checking if
F_{0}
is indeed a solution for
(G,k,\eta)
; this takes time
\sum_{i=0}^{k}\binom{{|{Z}|}}{i}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}=\binom{\mathcal{O}(k^{4})}{k}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}=2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. We then go over all pairs
(F_{0},(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\}))
that are good for
(G,k,\eta)
, and compute
\#\mathtt{del}(G-Z,(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
using Lemma
76
. As
F_{0}\subseteq E(G[Z])
and
{|{E(G[Z])}|}\leq\binom{{|{Z}|}}{2}
,
F_{0}
has at most
\sum_{i=0}^{k}\binom{\binom{{|{Z}|}}{2}}{i}=2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}
choices and
(Y,\{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}\})
has
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}
choices (by Lemma
51
), this step also takes time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. Notice that all the arithmetic operations we need to perform to compute
\#\mathtt{del}(G-Z,(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\}))
only involve numbers that can be encoded using
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
bits. We can therefore compute
{\#\mathtt{sol}}(G,k,\eta)
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. We thus have the following result.

Theorem 78
.

#BCD
admits an algorithm that runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

An algorithm for #BCE

To design an algorithm for
#BCE
, we follow the same strategy that we used for
#BCD
. But there are differences. We first discuss them, and outline our main arguments.

Idea behind our algorithm for
#BCE
.

Consider an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
#BCE
. As in the case of
#BCD
, we start with a subset of vertices
Z
with similar properties:
{|{Z}|}=k^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
,
G-Z
is a cluster graph and there are no edges between
Z
and
V(G)\setminus Z
. Every solution
F\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}
is then the disjoint union of three (possibly empty) parts,
F_{0}
,
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, where
F_{0}
is the intersection of
F
with
G[Z]
,
F_{1}
is the set of edges that we delete (apart from those in
F_{0}
) and
F_{2}
is the set of edges that we add (again, apart from those in
F_{0}
