). Notice that
G\triangle F_{0}
must be a cluster graph.
Now, to calculate
{\#\mathtt{sol}}(G,k,\eta)
, we go over all possible choices for
F_{0}
, and count the number of possibilities for
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
that are consistent with
F_{0}
. And as before, we associate
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
with sets of partitions of
{|{V(F_{1})}|}
and
{|{V(F_{2})}|}
.
But quite unlike in the case of
#BCD
, where for each
F_{0}
, the rest of the solution was “untouched” by
Z
(meaning completely contained in
G-Z
), we do not have that guarantee for
#BCE
, as we can now add edges to
G
(i.e.,
F_{2}
) and some of those edges could be incident with vertices in
Z
. So while counting the number of solutions we must ensure that no two components of
G[Z]\triangle F_{0}
are merged together when we add the edges in
F_{2}
. A component of
G[Z]\triangle F_{0}
, however, may be merged with a component of
G-Z
. Now, as we did in our algorithm for (the decision version of)
BCE
, we think of
G\triangle F
as being obtained from
G\triangle F_{0}
by a two step process: first by deleting the edges of
F_{1}
, and then adding the edges of
F_{2}
. Notice that
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are disjoint. So in this two step process,
F_{2}
does not merge together two components that were split by
F_{1}
; for example, consider any component
H
of
G-Z
that was split into smaller components
H_{1},H_{2},\ldots,H_{r}
when we deleted
F_{1}
; notice that
F_{2}
does not merge two
H_{i}
s together as
F_{1}\cap F_{2}=\emptyset
. We must take this fact into account while counting the number of choices for
F_{2}
. To deal with all these restrictions on certain pairs of components that cannot be merged together, we reduce the problem to
CRBCC
\lipicsEnd

Solving
#BCE

We are finally ready to design an algorithm for
#BCE
. The last ingredient we need for our algorithm is a set
Z\subseteq V(G)
that contains all the non-clique components of
G
. To find such a
Z
, we turn to our kernelization algorithm for
BCE
, and in particular, Reduction Rules
18
,
19
and
22
that we used to bound the number of vertices that belong to the non-clique components of
G
. Reduction Rules
18
and
19
respectively delete and add edges that belong to every solution, and therefore preserve the number of solutions, and Reduction Rule
22
simply eliminates no-instances. By exhaustively applying these three rules, we can derive the following result.

Lemma 79
.

There is an algorithm that given an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
#BCE
, runs in polynomial time, and either correctly reports that
(G,k,\eta)
is a no-instance of
BCE
, or returns an instance
(G^{\prime},k^{\prime},\eta)
of
#BCE
and a set
Z\subseteq V(G^{\prime})
such that
{\#\mathtt{sol}}(G,k,\eta)={\#\mathtt{sol}}(G^{\prime},k^{\prime},\eta)
,
{|{Z}|}=\mathcal{O}(k^{3})
,
G^{\prime}-Z
is a cluster graph, and there is no edge between
Z
and
V(G^{\prime})\setminus Z
(that is,
Z
contains all the non-clique components of
G^{\prime}
).

Consider an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
#BCE
. From now on, we assume that we have run the algorithm of Lemma
79
on our input instance and found the set
Z
; let us denote the resulting instance by
(G,k,\eta)
as well. Notice that each solution
F\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}
for
(G,k,\eta)
is the disjoint union of three sets
F_{0}
,
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, where
F_{0}=F\cap\binom{Z}{2}
is the intersection of
F
with
G[Z]
,
F_{1}=(F\cap E(G))\setminus F_{0}
is the edges that we delete from
G
(apart from those in
F_{0}
, and thus
F_{1}\subseteq E(G-Z)
), and
F_{2}=(F\cap\binom{V(G)}{2}\setminus E(G))\setminus F_{0}
is the set of edges that we add to
G
(apart from those in
F_{0}
).
As
F=F_{0}\cup F_{1}\cup F_{2}
is a solution, and
F_{1}\cup F_{2}
does not modify any (non-)edge with both its endpoints in
Z
, we can conclude that
G[Z]\triangle F_{0}
is cluster graph; this, along with the fact that
G-Z
is a cluster graph, implies that
G\triangle F_{0}
is indeed a cluster graph. Notice that we cannot, however, conclude that
F_{0}
is a solution for the instance
(G[Z],k,\eta)
, as
G[Z]\triangle F_{0}
need not be
\eta
-balanced. To count the number of solutions
F=F_{0}\cup F_{1}\cup F_{2}
for
(G,k,\eta)
, we will now group the solutions into four classes depending on whether or not
F_{1}
or
F_{2}
is empty, and we will show that the number of solutions in each class can be computed in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
In what follows, we will write
\bm{Y}
as a shorthand for
(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
.

1.

When
F_{1}\neq\emptyset
and
F_{2}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
A_{1}
be the number of solutions
F=F_{0}\cup F_{1}\cup F_{2}
for
(G,k,\eta)
such that
F_{1}\neq\emptyset
and
F_{2}\neq\emptyset
.
For
F_{0}\subseteq\binom{Z}{2}
and
\bm{Y}
, we say that the pair
(F_{0},\bm{Y})
is semi-good for
(G,k,\eta)
if the following conditions hold:

•

G\triangle F_{0}
is a cluster graph;

•

k-{|{F_{0}}|}\geq 1
;

•

(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
is such that
Y=\left\{{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{s}}\right\}
is a partition of
\ell
, for some
\ell\in[2(k-{|{F_{0}}|})]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\}
, and
Y_{i}
is a partition of
y_{i}
for every
i\in[s]
;

•

Y
is
(G-Z)
-valid;

•

\sum_{i\in[s]}\mathtt{spp}(Y_{i})\leq k-{|{F_{0}}|}
.

Consider a pair
(F_{0},\bm{Y})
that is semi-good for
(G,k,\eta)
. Based on
(F_{0},\bm{Y})
, we define an instance
I_{F_{0},\bm{Y}}=(G^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},k^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
of
CRBCC
as follows.
For
i\in[s]
, let
Y_{i}=\left\{{y_{i1},y_{i2},\ldots,y_{ir_{i}}}\right\}
. We first define a cluster graph
G^{\prime}
and a colouring function
{{\mu^{\prime}}:{\mathtt{CC}(G^{\prime})}\rightarrow{[s+1]_{0}}}
obtained from
G\triangle F_{0}
as follows. Fix
s
components
H_{1},H_{2},\ldots,H_{s}
of
G-Z
such that
{|{H_{i}}|}=x_{i}
for every
i\in[s]
; notice that the
H_{i}
s are components of
G\triangle F_{0}
as well. Let
G^{\prime}
be a deletion of
G\triangle F_{0}
w.r.t.
(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
in which each
H_{i}
is split into
r_{i}
components
H_{i1},H_{i2},\ldots,H_{ir_{i}}
such that
{|{H_{ij}}|}=y_{ij}
for every
j\in[r_{i}]
. We will define
\mu^{\prime}
in such a way that no two
H_{ij}
and
H_{ij^{\prime}}
can be merged together, and no two components of
G^{\prime}
that are fully contained in
Z
can be merged together either. With this in mind, we define
\mu^{\prime}(H_{ij})=i
for every
i\in[s]
,
j\in[r_{i}]
, and for every component
H\neq H_{ij}
for any
i\in[s]
,
j\in[r_{i}]
, we define
\mu^{\prime}(H)=s+1
if
V(H)\subseteq Z
and
\mu^{\prime}(H)=0
otherwise. Also, we define
k^{\prime}=k-{|{F_{0}}|}-\sum_{i\in[s]}\mathtt{spp}(Y_{i})
, and
\eta^{\prime}=\eta
.

It is straightforward to argue that
F=F_{0}\cup F_{1}\cup F_{2}
is solution for
(G,k,\eta)
with
F_{1},F_{2}\neq\emptyset
if and only if
{|{V(F_{1})}|}
can be associated with an appropriately defined
\bm{Y}
such that
(F_{0},\bm{Y})
is semi-good for
(G,k,\eta)
and
F_{2}
is a non-empty solution for the instance
I_{F_{0},\bm{Y}}
. We therefore have

A_{1}=\sum_{(F_{0},\bm{Y})}{\#\mathtt{sol}}^{\neq\emptyset}(I_{F_{0},\bm{Y}}),

where the summation is over all pairs
(F_{0},\bm{Y})
that are semi-good for
(G,k,\eta)
, and
{\#\mathtt{sol}}^{\neq\emptyset}(I_{F_{0},\bm{Y}})
is the number of non-empty solutions for the instance
I_{F_{0},\bm{Y}}
of
CRBCC
.
Also, we can compute
A_{1}
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. To see this, notice that as
s\leq{|{V(F_{1})}|}\leq 2k
, the function
\mu
uses
s+2
colours (including
0
). Therefore, by Lemma
73
, we can compute
{\#\mathtt{sol}}^{\neq\emptyset}(I_{F_{0},\bm{Y}})
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. And the number of choices for the pair
(F_{0},\bm{Y})
is
\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{\binom{{|{Z}|}}{2}}{j}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}={|{Z}|}^{\mathcal{O}(k)}\cdot 2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}=2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}
. We can therefore compute
A_{1}
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

2.

When
F_{1}=\emptyset
and
F_{2}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
A_{2}
be the number of solutions
F=F_{0}\cup F_{1}\cup F_{2}
for
(G,k,\eta)
such that
F_{1}=\emptyset
and
F_{2}\neq\emptyset
. As in the previous case, we will show that computing
A_{2}
amounts to solving an instance of
#CRBCC
. We define an instance
I_{F_{0}}=(G^{\prime\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime})
of
CRBCC
as follows:
G^{\prime\prime}=G\triangle F_{0}
,
k^{\prime\prime}=k-{|{F_{0}}|}
,
\eta^{\prime\prime}=\eta
, and for a component
H
of
G^{\prime\prime}
,
\mu^{\prime\prime}(H)=1
if
V(H)\subseteq Z
and
\mu^{\prime\prime}(H)=0
otherwise. Again, it is straightforward to argue that
F=F_{0}\cup F_{2}
with
F_{2}\neq\emptyset
is a solution for
(G,k,\eta)
if and only if
F_{2}
is a non-empty solution for the instance
I_{F_{0}}
of
CRBCC
. Therefore,

A_{2}=\sum_{F_{0}}{\#\mathtt{sol}}^{\neq\emptyset}(I_{F_{0}}),

where the summation is over all
F_{0}\subseteq\binom{Z}{2}
such that
G\triangle F_{0}
is a cluster graph and
{|{F_{0}}|}\leq k
. Now, as
\mu^{\prime\prime}
uses only
2
colours, by Lemma
73
, we can compute
{\#\mathtt{sol}}^{\neq\emptyset}(I_{F_{0}})
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. Also,
F_{0}
has
\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom{\binom{{|{Z}|}}{2}}{j}=2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}
choices. We can therefore compute
A_{2}
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

3.

When
F_{1}\neq\emptyset
and
F_{2}=\emptyset
.
Let
A_{3}
be the number of solutions
F=F_{0}\cup F_{1}\cup F_{2}
for
(G,k,\eta)
such that
F_{1}\neq\emptyset
and
F_{2}=\emptyset
. Notice that
F=F_{0}\cup F_{1}
with
F_{1}\neq\emptyset
is a solution for
(G,k,\eta)
if and only if
F_{1}
is a non-empty solution for the instance
(G\triangle F_{0},k-{|{F_{0}}|},\eta)
of
BCD
such that
F_{2}\cap E(G[Z]\triangle F_{0})=\emptyset
. It is then straightforward to associate
F_{1}
with
\bm{Y}
such that
(G-Z)-F_{1}
is a deletion of
G-Z
w.r.t.
\bm{Y}
. Therefore,

A_{3}=\sum_{(F_{0},\bm{Y})}\#\mathtt{del}(G-Z,\bm{Y}),

where the summation is over all pairs
(F_{0},\bm{Y})
such that

•

F_{0}\subseteq\binom{Z}{2}
and
G\triangle F_{0}
is a cluster graph,

•

k-{|{F_{0}}|}\geq 1
,

•

(Y,\left\{{Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{s}}\right\})
is such that
Y=\left\{{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{s}}\right\}
is a partition of
\ell
, for some
\ell\in[2(k-{|{F_{0}}|})]\setminus\left\{{1}\right\}
, and
Y_{i}
is a partition of
y_{i}
for every
i\in[s]
;

•

Y
is
(G-Z)
-valid;

•

(\mathtt{CS}(G\triangle F_{0})\setminus Y)\cup\hat{Y}
is
\eta
-balanced,
where
\hat{Y}=\bigcup_{i\in[s]}Y_{i}
;

•

\sum_{i\in[s]}\mathtt{spp}(Y_{i})\leq k-{|{F_{0}}|}
.

Again, we can argue that the number of choices for
(F_{0},\bm{Y})
is
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}
, and we can thus compute
A_{3}
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

4.

When
F_{1}=F_{2}=\emptyset
.
Let
A_{4}
be the number of solutions
F=F_{0}\cup F_{1}\cup F_{2}
for
(G,k,\eta)
such that
F_{1}=\emptyset
and
F_{2}\neq\emptyset
. Notice in this case that
F_{0}
must be a solution for the instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
BCE
. Thus, by going over all possible choices for
F_{0}
and checking if it is a solution, we can compute
A_{4}
. In particular,

A_{4}=\sum_{F_{0}}1
,
where the summation is over all
F_{0}\subseteq\binom{Z}{2}
such that
F_{0}
is a solution for
(G,k,\eta)
. As before,
F_{0}
has
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}
choices, and therefore, we can compute
A_{4}
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

We have shown that we can compute
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4}
, and consequently
{\#\mathtt{sol}}_{BCE}(G,k,\eta)=A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3}+A_{4}
, in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
. We thus have the following result.

Theorem 80
.

#BCE
admits an algorithm that runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(k\log k)}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

6
Conclusion

We studied variants of the widely studied
Cluster Editing
problem, and designed single-exponential time
FPT
algorithms and polynomial kernels for them. We also designed
FPT
algorithms for the counting versions of the problems. These results add to a growing body of literature on algorithms for clustering under size or balance constraints. Our work triggers several questions for future research. First, while our kernel for the completion version has
\mathcal{O}(k)
vertices, our kernels for the deletion and editing versions have
\mathcal{O}(k^{4})
and
\mathcal{O}(k^{3})
vertices, respectively. Can we design
\mathcal{O}(k)
kernels for these problems as well? Steinvik
[
55
]
also raised the same question. Second, it is known that
Cluster Editing
and
Cluster Deletion
do not admit sub-exponential time
FPT
algorithms unless the Exponential Time Hypothesis (ETH) fails
[
43
]
. This lower bound applies to
BCD
and
BCC
as well. But the case of
BCC
is open: Can we design a sub-exponential time
FPT
algorithm for
BCC
or prove that such an algorithm does not exist unless ETH fails? Third, can we extend our results to similar edge modification problems under balance constraints, for example, the bipartite counterpart of
Cluster Editing
, called
Bicluster Editing

[
33
,
60
]
and generalizations of
Cluster Editing
such as
s
-Plex Cluster Editing

[
34
]
? Fourth, apart from these theoretical questions, it would be interesting to evaluate the practical competitiveness of our algorithms, particularly the efficacy of the reduction rules that we use in our kernelization algorithms on real-world or synthetic instances.

Appendix A
NP
-hardness of
Balanced Cluster Completion

To show that
BCC
is
NP
-hard, we use a reduction due to Froese et al.
[
27
]
, which shows that a related problem called
Cluster Transformation by Edge Addition
is
NP
-hard. In
Cluster Transformation by Edge Addition
, we are given a cluster graph
G
and a non-negative integer
k
, and we have to decide if we can add
exactly

k
edges to
G
in such a way that the resulting graph is also a cluster graph.
Froese et al.
[
27
]
showed that
Cluster Transformation by Edge Addition
is
NP
-hard by designing a reduction from a problem called
Numerical 3D-Matching
. We will argue that this reduction also shows the
NP
-hardness of
BCC
. Below, we reproduce the reduction from
[
27
]
and outline its correctness.

In
Numerical 3D-Matching
, which is known to be strongly
NP
-hard
[
29
]
, we are given positive integers
t
,
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
,
b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{n}
and
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots,c_{n}
, and we have to decide if there exist bijections
{{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}:{[n]}\rightarrow{[n]}}
such that
a_{\alpha(i)}+b_{\beta(i)}+c_{\gamma(i)}=t
for every
i\in[n]
.
We reduce
Numerical 3D-Matching
to
BCC
. Informally, the reduction works as follows. Given an instance of
Numerical 3D-Matching
, we construct a cluster graph
G
by introducing a “small” clique corresponding to each
a_{i}
, a “medium” clique corresponding to each
b_{i}
and a “large” clique corresponding to each
c_{i}
. We set
k
appropriately so that the only way we can turn
G
into a
0
-balanced cluster graph by adding at most
k
is by merging together one small clique, one medium clique and one large clique.

Consider an instance
I=(t,(a_{i})_{i\in[n]},(b_{i})_{i\in[n]},(c_{i})_{i\in[n]})
of
Numerical 3D-Matching
. As the problem is strongly
NP
-hard, we assume without loss of generality that
a_{i},b_{i},c_{i}\leq n^{d}
for some constant
d
. We also assume that
t>a_{i},b_{i},c_{i}
for every
i\in[n]
and that
\sum_{i\in[t]}a_{i}+b_{i}+c_{i}=nt
, as otherwise
I
is clearly a no-instance.

We construct an instance
(G,k,\eta)
of
BCC
as follows. First of all, we set
\eta=0
. Now, let
A=n^{2d}
,
B=n^{3d}
and
C=n^{7d}
. For
i\in[n]
, let
a^{\prime}_{i}=a_{i}+A
,
b^{\prime}_{i}=b_{i}+B
and
c^{\prime}_{i}=c_{i}+C
, and we add three cliques of sizes
a^{\prime}_{i},b^{\prime}_{i}
and
c^{\prime}_{i}
to
G
; we call these cliques small, medium and large, respectively. For convenience, we refer to these cliques by their sizes; for example, we may refer to the clique
a^{\prime}_{i}
. Let
t^{\prime}=t+A+B+C
, and we set

k=n\binom{t^{\prime}}{2}-{|{E(G)}|}=n\binom{t^{\prime}}{2}-\sum_{i\in[n]}\left({\binom{a^{\prime}_{i}}{2}+\binom{b^{\prime}_{i}}{2}+\binom{c^{\prime}_{i}}{2}}\right).

We now argue that
I
is a yes-instance of
Numerical 3D-Matching
if and only if
(G,k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCC
. The forward direction is straightforward. Assume that
I
is a yes-instance, and let
{{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}:{[n]}\rightarrow{[n]}}
be bijections such that
a_{\alpha(i)}+b_{\beta(i)}+c_{\gamma(i)}=t
for every
i\in[n]
. Then the cluster graph
G^{\prime}
obtained from
G
by merging the three cliques
a^{\prime}_{\alpha(i)}
,
b^{\prime}_{\beta(i)}
and
c^{\prime}_{\gamma(i)}
for each
i\in[n]
is
0
-balanced. And it is straightforward to verify that
{|{E(G^{\prime})\setminus E(G)}|}=k
.

The backward direction is more involved. Assume that
(G^{\prime},k,\eta)
is a yes-instance of
BCC
, and let
F\subseteq\binom{V(G)}{2}
be a solution for
(G,k,\eta)
. As
\eta=0
, the cluster graph
G+F
is
0
-balanced. To show that
I
is a yes-instance, we use the following sequence of arguments.

1.

For
n\geq 3
and
d\geq 1
, we have
n^{10d+1}\leq k\leq 2n^{d+1}

[
27
, Lemma 7]
.

2.

As a consequence, no two large cliques can be merged together, because each large clique has size
\Omega(C)=\Omega(n^{7d})
, and merging together two large cliques needs the addition of
\Omega(n^{14})
edges
[
27
, Lemma 8]
.

3.

This implies that the graph
G+F
has at least
n
components (one corresponding to each large clique of
G
).

4.

As
G+F
is
0
-balanced, we must merge each small clique and each medium clique with a large clique. To see this, notice that the total number of vertices in all the small and medium cliques together is
\sum_{i\in[n]}(a^{\prime}_{i}+b^{\prime}_{i})=\sum_{i\in[n]}(a_{i}+A+b_{i}+B)=nA+nB+\sum_{i\in[n]}(a_{i}+b_{i})=\mathcal{O}(n^{2d+1}+n^{3d+1}+n^{d+1})=\mathcal{O}(n^{3d+1})
, whereas each large clique has size
\Omega(C)=\Omega(n^{7d})
. Item
2
now implies that each small clique is merged with exactly one large clique, and similarly each medium clique is merged with exactly one large clique.

5.

Items
3
and
4
together imply that
G+F
has exactly
n
components.

6.

As
{|{G+F}|}={|{G}|}=\sum_{i\in[n]}(a^{\prime}_{i}+b^{\prime}_{i}+c^{\prime}_{i})=\sum_{i\in[n]}(a_{i}+A+b_{i}+B+c_{i}+C)=n(A+B+C+t)=nt^{\prime}
, and
G+F
is
0
-balanced with exactly
n
components, we can conclude that each component of
G+F
has size exactly
t^{\prime}
.

7.

But then, as
t^{\prime}=t+A+B+C=t+n^{2d}+n^{3d}+n^{7d}
and
t\leq 3n^{d}
, each component of
G+F
must be formed by merging together one small, one medium and one large clique
[
27
, Lemma 13]
. In other words, there exist bijections
{{\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}}:{[n]}\rightarrow{[n]}}
such that for each
i\in[n]
, we have
a^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}(i)}+b^{\prime}_{\beta^{\prime}(i)}+c^{\prime}_{\gamma^{\prime}(i)}=t^{\prime}

8.

Based on Item
7
, it is straightforward to argue that
I
is a yes-instance
[
27
, Proof of Theorem 6]
. For each
i\in[n]
, we have
a_{\alpha^{\prime}(i)}+b_{\beta^{\prime}(i)}+c_{\gamma^{\prime}(i)}=a^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\prime}(i)}-A+b^{\prime}_{\beta^{\prime}(i)}-B+c^{\prime}_{\gamma^{\prime}(i)}-C=t^{\prime}-A-B-C=t
. This shows that
I
is a yes-instance of
Numerical 3D-Matching
.
